# Recep Tayyip Erdoğan — Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Overview

Recep Tayyip Erdoğan was Türkiye’s president throughout 2023 and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Parti). He won the 28 May presidential runoff, while the AK Parti-led People’s Alliance retained a parliamentary majority after the 14 May elections. The presidential system concentrated executive authority in the presidency; Erdoğan therefore had strong influence over government policy, senior appointments, security institutions, foreign policy, and public administration.

Important limits remained: parliamentary approval was required for legislation and some international commitments; courts and constitutional institutions retained formal powers; coalition partners, especially the Nationalist Movement Party (MHP), mattered politically; markets, NATO, Russia, the EU, and earthquake-related international assistance constrained choices; and municipal governments retained local responsibilities. In practice, the European Commission assessed executive checks and balances as ineffective, with accountability operating primarily through elections.

The central events of 2023 were the February earthquakes, the presidential and parliamentary elections, continued military operations in Syria and Iraq, the collapse of the Black Sea Grain Initiative, Sweden’s NATO accession process, and a late-year shift toward tighter monetary policy after years of unorthodox economic management.

The principal underlying source opened and extracted below is the European Commission’s *Türkiye Report 2023*, which covers June 2022–June 2023 and includes government, international-organization, NGO, and comparative-index evidence. It is especially useful for institutions, rights, elections, public administration, corruption, migration, security, and foreign policy. [European Commission, *Türkiye Report 2023*](https://ab.gov.tr/siteimages/birimler/kpb/trkiye_report_2023.pdf)

## 2. Evidence records

### Record ER-01 — Erdoğan re-elected amid an uneven electoral field

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye held presidential and parliamentary elections on 14 May 2023 and a presidential runoff on 28 May. Erdoğan was re-elected, and the ruling coalition retained a parliamentary majority. The European Commission found that voters had genuine alternatives and participation was high, but biased media coverage and unequal campaign conditions gave the incumbent an unjustified advantage. This establishes both electoral legitimacy and important limits on the competitiveness of the contest.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, sections 2.1.1 “Democracy” and summary, pp. 3–5. The report states that the incumbent was re-elected, the ruling coalition retained its majority, and media imbalance disadvantaged the incumbent.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Strong. The presidential election directly determined his continuation in office; the media and administrative environment operated under the presidential system he led.

**Complicating evidence.** The election offered meaningful opposition choices, voter turnout was high, and Erdoğan’s victory required a second round rather than occurring without competition.

**Cautions.** The Commission is an external institutional evaluator rather than an election adjudicator. Its reporting period ends in June 2023, so later developments are outside its main evidentiary window.

**Independent support.** OSCE/ODIHR’s 2023 election observation report; International Republican Institute election materials; Turkish Supreme Election Council results; Associated Press reporting on the opposition campaign.

### Record ER-02 — Presidential power was highly centralized

**Claim and significance.** The constitutional structure concentrated policymaking and regulatory authority in the presidency and did not provide an effective separation of powers. The European Commission reported that the executive was democratically accountable mainly through elections; most regulatory authorities remained linked to the presidency; and opposition mayors and parliamentarians faced pressure. This is central to assessing Erdoğan’s actual influence and attribution of government outcomes.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, pp. 4–5, sections on democratic institutions, public administration, and judicial independence.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns the 2022–June 2023 reporting period and the 2023 post-election system.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Strong. The system was built around the presidency Erdoğan occupied, although institutional arrangements also reflected reforms enacted before 2023.

**Complicating evidence.** Parliament remained electorally competitive and coalition support was politically necessary. Courts and administrative bodies retained formal authority.

**Cautions.** Institutional assessments can understate informal bargaining within the ruling coalition and should be paired with constitutional texts, parliamentary records, and court decisions.

**Independent support.** Bertelsmann Stiftung, *BTI 2024 Country Report: Türkiye*; Venice Commission opinions; UK House of Commons Library, *Turkey under Erdoğan: Recent Developments and the 2023 Elections*.

### Record ER-03 — Earthquake destruction exposed public-safety and governance failures

**Claim and significance.** Two powerful earthquakes and aftershocks struck southeastern Türkiye in February 2023, killing tens of thousands and destroying homes, schools, health facilities, and other infrastructure. The government’s central priorities after the election included relief and reconstruction. The disaster created a major test of state capacity and raised questions about building-code enforcement, emergency coordination, procurement, and accountability.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, pp. 3 and 6–7; it records the scale of destruction and the international donors’ conference, which raised more than €6 billion.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Moderate to strong for response, reconstruction, and national regulatory policy; weaker for each individual building failure because responsibility was distributed among municipalities, contractors, inspectors, ministries, and prior administrations.

**Complicating evidence.** The state coordinated international assistance and began reconstruction; civil-society organizations also provided substantial relief despite pressure.

**Cautions.** Early casualty, damage, and response figures changed over time. Disaster attribution requires engineering and administrative records, not only political reporting.

**Independent support.** Associated Press reporting on shelter, sanitation, family separation, reconstruction, building-code enforcement, and investigations; academic earthquake engineering studies; World Bank and UN recovery assessments.

### Record ER-04 — Security policy combined genuine threats with weak accountability

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye faced continuing PKK-related security risks and conducted military and air operations in northern Syria and Iraq. The European Commission recognized the state’s responsibility to combat terrorism but warned that operations must comply with human-rights and rule-of-law standards. It also found that civilian oversight of security forces remained incomplete and parliamentary accountability mechanisms were weak.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, pp. 4–5, sections on security, civilian oversight, and fundamental rights.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns the reporting period and 2023 operations.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Strong for strategic authorization and executive direction; operational responsibility was shared with the armed forces and security ministries.

**Complicating evidence.** PKK attacks and border threats were real, and the Commission acknowledged the government’s legitimate duty to protect the population.

**Cautions.** Conflict-zone casualty and attribution data are difficult to verify; Turkish authorities, Kurdish groups, Syrian actors, and international monitors provide contested accounts.

**Independent support.** UN OHCHR reporting on civilian deaths in Syria; ACLED’s analysis of the Kurdish question and election-period violence; Turkish parliamentary transcripts; conflict-event datasets.

### Record ER-05 — Democratic and civil-liberties backsliding continued

**Claim and significance.** The Commission found continuing deterioration in freedom of expression, assembly, association, judicial independence, and treatment of journalists, lawyers, academics, human-rights defenders, and opposition voices. Demonstrations were repeatedly restricted, sometimes met with disproportionate force, and followed by investigations, prosecutions, or fines. The election campaign also suffered from non-independent and imbalanced media coverage.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, pp. 5–7, sections on judiciary, fundamental rights, expression, assembly, and elections.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2022–June 2023, with broader structural conditions inherited from earlier years.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Strong at the policy and institutional level, though individual prosecutions and police actions require case-specific attribution.

**Complicating evidence.** Opposition parties remained active, elections provided genuine alternatives, and civil society continued earthquake-relief work.

**Cautions.** International monitoring may emphasize rights violations more than ordinary administrative functioning; government claims and security rationales should be retained alongside critical evidence.

**Independent support.** Constitutional Court decisions; Committee to Protect Journalists’ Türkiye archive; European Court of Human Rights judgments; Freedom House and Reporters Without Borders assessments.

### Record ER-06 — Corruption controls and public accountability remained weak

**Claim and significance.** The Commission assessed Türkiye as at an early stage in combating corruption and found no progress during the reporting period. It identified gaps in prevention policy, institutional independence, transparency, public accountability, and safeguards against undue influence in investigations and prosecutions. This is relevant to Erdoğan’s personal-integrity and governing-effectiveness assessments, but it does not by itself prove personal corruption.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, pp. 5–6, sections on corruption and public administration.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns the reporting period; some institutional weaknesses predated 2023.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Moderate. The executive and presidency shaped the institutional environment, but a ruler-level finding requires evidence involving specific decisions, associates, assets, contracts, or investigations.

**Complicating evidence.** Türkiye participated in international anti-corruption frameworks and conducted some law-enforcement operations against organized crime.

**Cautions.** Perception indices measure broad national conditions, not an individual’s conduct. Opposition allegations and media investigations require documentary corroboration.

**Independent support.** GRECO evaluations; Transparency International’s 2023 CPI; World Bank Worldwide Governance Indicators; Turkish audit and procurement records; opposition-party reports.

### Record ER-07 — Economic policy shifted toward stabilization after severe imbalances

**Claim and significance.** Erdoğan’s government entered 2023 with high inflation, exchange-rate instability, and concerns about unconventional monetary policy. After the election, the government moved toward more orthodox stabilization, including substantial central-bank rate increases. The policy shift was intended to restore financial stability but imposed short-term costs through tighter credit and reduced purchasing power.

**Strongest source.** The European Commission’s economic chapter in *Türkiye 2023 Report*, pp. 60–70 and 99–100, should be paired with the IMF’s 2023 Article IV consultation and Central Bank of the Republic of Türkiye decisions.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, with the economic conditions partly inherited from earlier policy.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Strong for broad economic direction and appointment authority; weaker for day-to-day central-bank decisions after the post-election institutional reset.

**Complicating evidence.** The government preserved growth and employment in parts of the economy, and later monetary tightening reflected an attempt to correct earlier instability.

**Cautions.** Nominal-dollar indicators cannot establish real growth or living-standard improvement. Inflation statistics were politically contested and should be compared with independent estimates and household data.

**Independent support.** IMF Article IV report; World Bank WDI; Central Bank policy statements; TÜİK statistics; academic work on Turkish inflation and private debt; Associated Press reporting on rate increases.

### Record ER-08 — Erdoğan pursued transactional diplomacy with Russia, Ukraine, NATO, and the EU

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye condemned Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and helped facilitate the Black Sea Grain Initiative and prisoner exchanges, but did not join Western sanctions and deepened economic ties with Russia, especially in energy. The grain arrangement ended after Russia withdrew in July 2023. Erdoğan also submitted Sweden’s NATO accession protocol to parliament in October. This demonstrates substantial diplomatic agency combined with balancing constraints.

**Strongest source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, pp. 7–8 and 123–130, sections on foreign, security, and defence policy.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to Erdoğan.** Strong. The presidency directed foreign-policy positioning, NATO negotiations, mediation efforts, and relations with Russia and Ukraine.

**Complicating evidence.** Türkiye’s mediation and grain diplomacy produced internationally significant benefits; it also implemented a ban on certain sanctioned goods transiting toward Russia.

**Cautions.** The Commission noted both cooperation and unresolved concerns about dual-use goods and sanctions circumvention. Foreign-policy assessments differ according to whether strategic autonomy or alignment with EU policy is treated as the benchmark.

**Independent support.** UN and Turkish Grain Initiative documents; NATO records; Carnegie Endowment analyses; Associated Press reporting; Turkish parliamentary ratification records.

## 3. Information environment and evidence limitations

Reliable information was available from international organizations, election observers, courts, central-bank records, parliamentary documents, and high-quality reporting, but access and interpretation were difficult.

The main limitations were:

- strong executive influence over media and regulatory institutions;
- prosecutions and administrative pressure affecting journalists, lawyers, academics, activists, and opposition figures;
- restrictions on demonstrations and terrorism-related legal provisions that can chill reporting and civic participation;
- politically polarized claims about the earthquake response, election fairness, inflation, and security operations;
- incomplete civilian and parliamentary oversight of security institutions;
- contested casualty, displacement, building-failure, and conflict-event figures;
- weak transparency in corruption investigations and public administration;
- difficulty separating Erdoğan’s personal decisions from actions by ministries, municipalities, contractors, coalition partners, courts, and security agencies;
- national indicators that describe Türkiye’s condition but do not automatically establish ruler attribution.

The European Commission report is valuable because it identifies its source base and covers multiple institutional and civil-society perspectives, but it is not a substitute for primary Turkish records or case-level documentation.

## 4. Questions for deeper research

1. What decisions by Erdoğan, ministries, municipalities, contractors, and inspectors most directly contributed to earthquake deaths and reconstruction outcomes?
2. How much television, state advertising, public-resource, and judicial advantage did the incumbent receive during the 2023 election?
3. What specific policies drove inflation and currency instability, and how quickly did the post-election stabilization program improve real household welfare?
4. Which 2023 security operations in Syria and Iraq produced verified civilian harm?
5. What concrete cases demonstrate executive interference in courts, prosecutors, corruption investigations, or opposition municipalities?
6. How did MHP and other coalition partners constrain or redirect Erdoğan’s domestic and security agenda?
7. What was the measurable effect of refugee, education, health, and social-assistance policies on citizens and refugees?
8. Did the Sweden NATO process produce durable strategic gains or mainly reflect bargaining with NATO allies?

## 5. Corroborating sources opened and useful

- European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report* — fully opened and extracted.
- Bertelsmann Stiftung, *BTI 2024 Country Report: Türkiye* — opened as a comparative governance source.
- Associated Press reports in the supplied catalogue on elections, earthquake response, building-code enforcement, grain shipments, NATO, and monetary policy — useful for corroboration; each should be reopened and extracted at article level before score-bearing use.
- Constitutional Court press release on expression and assembly rights — promising primary legal corroboration.
- IMF, *Republic of Türkiye: 2023 Article IV Consultation* — needed for primary macroeconomic corroboration.
- OSCE/ODIHR 2023 election observation materials — needed for detailed election administration and media findings.
- UNHCR, UNICEF, UN OHCHR, World Bank, and IAEA materials in the catalogue — useful for refugee protection, children, conflict, reconstruction, and nuclear-policy corroboration.

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- European Commission report sections on economic policy, social policy, media, education, and energy.
- OSCE/ODIHR final election report and Turkish Supreme Election Council results.
- IMF Article IV consultation and Central Bank rate decisions from June–December 2023.
- Turkish Court of Accounts, procurement, and earthquake reconstruction records.
- AP investigation into pre-earthquake building-code enforcement and the 612-person investigation.
- UN and engineering assessments of earthquake mortality, building collapse, and recovery.
- NATO and Turkish parliamentary records on Sweden’s accession protocol.
- Primary UN/Türkiye documents on the Black Sea Grain Initiative.
- Specific Constitutional Court and ECtHR judgments involving opposition politicians, journalists, assembly, and expression.
- GRECO and Transparency International materials for the corruption-control record.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Erdoğan was Türkiye’s president throughout 2023 and, under the presidential system, the principal authority setting national energy, nuclear, defence, foreign-policy, and security priorities. Nuclear-specific responsibilities were delegated to the Nuclear Regulatory Authority (NDK), Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources, TENMAK, the armed forces, and other agencies. Erdoğan retained substantial appointment, policy-setting, treaty, and strategic authority, but did not control all relevant systems: U.S. nuclear weapons at Incirlik remained under U.S. custody; NATO nuclear policy required alliance consultation; IAEA safeguards and international agreements constrained Türkiye; and technical regulation depended on NDK and external expertise.

Türkiye had no indigenous nuclear weapons arsenal. Its principal nuclear-risk exposure was instead:

- participation in NATO nuclear-sharing arrangements;
- U.S. B61 bombs reportedly stored at Incirlik;
- construction and initial operationalization of the Russian-built Akkuyu nuclear power plant;
- safeguards, emergency preparedness, spent-fuel, and waste-management capacity;
- dependence on Russia for nuclear technology and plant operation;
- conventional crises involving Russia, Syria, Iraq, Ukraine, NATO, and the Eastern Mediterranean;
- cyber and other dual-use risks.

The strongest 2023 evidence shows formal nonproliferation and safety commitments, continued participation in NATO nuclear arrangements, expansion of civilian nuclear infrastructure, and unresolved questions about regulatory independence, Russian dependence, liability, and transparency. The evidence does not establish that Erdoğan sought nuclear weapons or issued nuclear-use threats in 2023.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-1B-01 | Türkiye remained a non-nuclear-weapon state with IAEA safeguards and an Additional Protocol | 1B.4, 1B.6, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-02 | Türkiye joined the Joint Convention on spent-fuel and radioactive-waste management in February 2023 | 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-03 | Incirlik reportedly hosted 20–30 U.S. B61 bombs, with Türkiye participating in NATO’s nuclear mission | 1B.3, 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-04 | Erdoğan’s government advanced Akkuyu, a Russian-built nuclear plant, while increasing long-term Russian technical and energy dependence | 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-05 | Türkiye’s nuclear regulatory framework gave NDK broad responsibilities, but its institutional independence and accountability require scrutiny | 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-06 | Türkiye’s 2023 foreign policy combined de-escalatory mediation with military operations and continued exposure to major-power crises | 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-07 | The 2020–2023 national cybersecurity strategy provided a formal cyber-risk framework, but implementation evidence for 2023 remains incomplete | 1B.9, 1B.10 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 1B.1 — Reducing existential risk

**Disposition: partially evidenced; mixed record.**

Formal nonproliferation, safeguards, spent-fuel, and nuclear-safety commitments point toward risk reduction. Türkiye also participated in diplomatic mediation over Ukraine and supported the Black Sea Grain Initiative.

Countervailing evidence is the expansion of Akkuyu under a model giving Russia a central role in construction, operation, fuel, and technical support, alongside continued participation in NATO’s nuclear mission. The record does not establish that these choices were intended to increase existential risk, but they created strategic dependence and exposure that require deeper assessment.

Evidence: WEB-1B-01, WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04, WEB-1B-06.

### 1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric

**Disposition: limited direct evidence.**

No inspected source established that Erdoğan made a nuclear-use threat, advocated nuclear first use, or used apocalyptic nuclear rhetoric during 2023. This absence should not be treated as proof of responsible rhetoric. The supplied catalogue includes a November 2023 Anadolu Agency report in which Erdoğan called for investigation of Israel’s nuclear weapons; it remains useful for rhetoric research but was not opened in this pass.

Evidence gap: direct 2023 speeches, transcripts, UN statements, NATO statements, and Turkish-language media should be checked.

### 1B.3 — Safe nuclear control

**Disposition: mixed and incomplete.**

Türkiye was subject to IAEA safeguards and had formal national regulatory responsibilities through NDK. NATO nuclear-sharing arrangements reportedly maintained U.S. custody and control of B61 weapons at Incirlik, limiting Erdoğan’s unilateral authority over those weapons.

The main unresolved issue is whether Türkiye had sufficient independent regulatory capacity, emergency preparedness, trained personnel, inspection resources, and public accountability as Akkuyu entered operation. The existence of a legal framework does not demonstrate effective implementation.

Evidence: WEB-1B-01, WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04, WEB-1B-05.

### 1B.4 — Arms control and inspections

**Disposition: substantially evidenced for formal commitments; implementation incomplete.**

IAEA records show Türkiye had an NPT safeguards agreement and Additional Protocol in force. It also acceded to the Joint Convention on the Safety of Spent Fuel Management and on the Safety of Radioactive Waste Management in February 2023. These are concrete formal commitments.

The inspected sources did not establish the quality of 2023 implementation, inspection access, corrective actions, or national reporting in practice.

Evidence: WEB-1B-01 and WEB-1B-02.

### 1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression

**Disposition: no direct adverse evidence established.**

Türkiye’s NATO nuclear role and reported B61 presence created an indirect relationship between nuclear deterrence and conventional military posture. However, the inspected evidence did not show Erdoğan using nuclear weapons or nuclear threats to authorize or shield Turkish operations in Syria, Iraq, Ukraine-related diplomacy, or the Eastern Mediterranean.

This lens requires caution: non-nuclear rhetoric cannot be inferred from silence, and the operational details of NATO nuclear planning are classified.

Evidence: WEB-1B-03 and WEB-1B-06; important unanswered question.

### 1B.6 — Preventing proliferation

**Disposition: formal safeguards evidenced; enforcement evidence limited.**

Türkiye had NPT safeguards and an Additional Protocol, and the IAEA treaty record identifies relevant physical-protection, early-notification, nuclear-safety, and waste-management commitments. No inspected source established Turkish assistance to nuclear proliferation in 2023.

Further work should examine export-control enforcement, dual-use trade with Russia, customs and sanctions-evasion investigations, and Türkiye’s implementation of UN Security Council obligations.

Evidence: WEB-1B-01; related gap under WEB-1B-06.

### 1B.7 — Independent risk expertise

**Disposition: institutionally evidenced but not yet outcome-tested.**

The nuclear governance framework assigned responsibilities to NDK, TENMAK, ministries, TÜBİTAK, universities, and international technical bodies. Türkiye also participated in the Multinational Design Evaluation Programme and related nuclear-safety cooperation.

The available evidence does not establish whether experts could dissent publicly, whether appointments were merit-based, or whether NDK could act independently against politically important projects such as Akkuyu.

Evidence: WEB-1B-05.

### 1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation

**Disposition: mixed.**

Türkiye used diplomatic channels in the Russia–Ukraine crisis, facilitated grain exports and prisoner exchanges, and sought talks and de-escalation. This is relevant to preventing escalation involving a nuclear-armed adversary.

At the same time, Türkiye continued military operations in Syria and Iraq and maintained a significant NATO nuclear role. No inspected source documented nuclear command-and-control decisions during a 2023 crisis.

Evidence: WEB-1B-03 and WEB-1B-06.

### 1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks

**Disposition: formal policy identified; operational evidence insufficient.**

The catalogue identifies a 2020–2023 National Cyber Security Strategy and Action Plan. It is relevant to cyber-risk governance, but I could not open the document in this pass. No reliable 2023 evidence was extracted on implementation, funding, independent audits, biological-security governance, AI controls, or correction of cyber vulnerabilities.

Evidence gap: WEB-1B-07 is discovery/context only until the strategy and 2023 implementation records are opened.

### 1B.10 — Lasting risk posture

**Disposition: unresolved; mixed inherited and new conditions.**

Türkiye ended 2023 with stronger formal nuclear-safety and waste-management commitments and an advancing civilian nuclear program. It also retained NATO nuclear-sharing exposure and deepened dependence on Russia through Akkuyu and energy ties. The available evidence does not permit a firm factual conclusion that the country’s overall catastrophic-risk posture was safer or less safe than at the beginning of the year.

Evidence: WEB-1B-01 through WEB-1B-07.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-1B-01 — IAEA safeguards and nonproliferation status

**Claim.** Türkiye was an IAEA member, party to the NPT safeguards system, and had an Additional Protocol in force. The IAEA treaty record also lists Türkiye as party to the Convention on the Physical Protection of Nuclear Material and its amendment, the Early Notification and Assistance Conventions, and the Convention on Nuclear Safety.

**Why it matters.** These are the principal formal safeguards and nonproliferation constraints on Türkiye’s nuclear activities.

**Source.** *Türkiye, Republic of — IAEA Country List and Treaty Status*, IAEA Office of Legal Affairs, current treaty-status page.

**Locator.** “Safeguards Agreements” and “Agreements” tables: Türkiye’s NPT safeguards agreement; Additional Protocol; CPPNM; CPPNM Amendment; Early Notification; Assistance; Convention on Nuclear Safety.

**Period fit.** Inherited formal baseline, operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Institutional attribution to the Turkish state; Erdoğan’s attribution is indirect but significant because the presidency set national policy and controlled the executive branch during 2023.

**Limitations.** Treaty status confirms formal obligations, not implementation quality or inspection effectiveness.

**Contrary evidence.** No inspected source showed treaty withdrawal or a 2023 safeguards violation.

**Corroboration.** IAEA safeguards records; UN NPT documents; CTBTO country materials, still needing inspection.

### WEB-1B-02 — Accession to the Joint Convention on spent fuel and radioactive waste

**Claim.** Türkiye acceded to the Joint Convention on the Safety of Spent Fuel Management and on the Safety of Radioactive Waste Management on 20 February 2023; it entered into force for Türkiye on 21 May 2023.

**Why it matters.** This was a concrete 2023 formal act strengthening the international framework applicable to Türkiye’s expanding nuclear program, including Akkuyu-related spent-fuel and waste-management responsibilities.

**Source.** *Türkiye, Republic of — IAEA Country List and Treaty Status*, IAEA Office of Legal Affairs.

**Locator.** “Agreements” table, Joint Convention entry: accession 2023-02-20; in force 2023-05-21.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Attributable to the Turkish state and Erdoğan’s executive government, though the accession process may have involved ministries and prior legislative preparation.

**Limitations.** The page does not show domestic implementation, budgets, staffing, inspections, or whether deficiencies were corrected.

**Contrary evidence.** No inspected contrary evidence.

**Corroboration.** IAEA Joint Convention documentation and Türkiye’s national report should be opened.

### WEB-1B-03 — NATO nuclear-sharing exposure at Incirlik

**Claim.** A 2023 Federation of American Scientists/Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists assessment estimated that Incirlik Air Base hosted 20–30 U.S. B61 nuclear bombs for delivery by U.S. aircraft. It reported that Türkiye contributed dual-capable aircraft to NATO’s nuclear mission and that U.S. custody and launch authority remained decisive.

**Why it matters.** Türkiye was not simply a non-nuclear state outside nuclear risk. Its territory and military infrastructure were associated with NATO nuclear deterrence, creating accident, escalation, security, and crisis-management exposure.

**Source.** Hans M. Kristensen, Matt Korda, Eliana Johns, and Mackenzie Knight, “Nuclear weapons sharing, 2023,” *Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists*, 7 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 393–398; Incirlik section, especially pp. 396–398, lines describing 20–30 B61 bombs, the security perimeter, the July 2023 “surety mission,” and strategic-deterrence role.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Türkiye’s participation was a state and NATO policy, not a unilateral Erdoğan decision. Erdoğan’s influence was substantial through control of defence and foreign policy, but U.S. custody and NATO procedures limited unilateral Turkish authority.

**Limitations.** The bomb estimate and base status are based partly on open-source analysis and satellite imagery; nuclear deployments are classified.

**Contrary evidence.** The source itself notes previous U.S. consideration of removing weapons because of security concerns, and uncertainty about operational details.

**Corroboration.** NATO nuclear-policy documents; U.S. Department of Defense budget and base-security records; Turkish and U.S. parliamentary records.

### WEB-1B-04 — Akkuyu and Russian nuclear dependence

**Claim.** Türkiye advanced the Akkuyu Nuclear Power Plant under a 2010 Türkiye–Russia cooperation agreement. A 2024 scholarly analysis records that Russia was particularly significant among Türkiye’s bilateral nuclear partners and that a Turkish NDK–Rostekhnadzor memorandum was concluded in early 2023 before Akkuyu’s operational phase. The same analysis describes the presidential system as giving the president substantial authority over nuclear policy.

**Why it matters.** Akkuyu expanded Türkiye’s civilian nuclear capacity but also created long-term dependence on Russia for technology, construction, operation, fuel, and technical support. This is relevant to strategic autonomy, safety oversight, crisis exposure, and the possibility that energy infrastructure becomes entangled with geopolitical leverage.

**Source.** “Türkiye’s Nuclear Energy Aspirations: Policy Challenges and Legal Trajectory,” *Journal of World Energy Law & Business*, Oxford Academic, 2024.

**Locator.** Lines 79–87 on international cooperation and the 2023 NDK–Rostekhnadzor memorandum; lines 88–104 on presidential authority, NDK, TENMAK, Akkuyu-related institutions, and nuclear governance.

**Period fit.** 2023 evidence with later retrospective publication.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the presidential policy environment and state-level strategic direction; individual contractual decisions require primary agreement and procurement records.

**Limitations.** Scholarly retrospective analysis is not a substitute for the original 2010 agreement, 2023 operating records, licensing documents, or Turkish budget records.

**Contrary evidence.** International technical cooperation and NDK’s membership in multilateral safety programs may reduce dependence-related risk.

**Corroboration.** IAEA country nuclear profile; Turkish Ministry of Energy activity report; Akkuyu project documents; Russian and Turkish regulatory records.

### WEB-1B-05 — Nuclear regulator and expert institutions

**Claim.** Türkiye’s Nuclear Regulatory Authority (NDK) had a broad statutory mandate covering radiation protection, nuclear security, safeguards, the lifecycle of nuclear installations, nuclear-material controls, emergency response, and personnel qualification. TENMAK retained nuclear research responsibilities, while NÜKEN, TÜBİTAK, universities, and international networks supplied technical capacity.

**Why it matters.** Effective nuclear safety depends on independent regulators, qualified staff, inspections, emergency planning, and the ability to challenge politically important projects.

**Source.** “Türkiye’s Nuclear Energy Aspirations: Policy Challenges and Legal Trajectory,” *Journal of World Energy Law & Business*, Oxford Academic, 2024.

**Locator.** Lines 88–104 and especially 93–98 on Presidential Decree No. 95, Law No. 7381, NDK’s mandate, NÜTED technical support, and TENMAK/NÜKEN.

**Period fit.** Institutional baseline operative during 2023; retrospective source.

**Ruler attribution.** Erdoğan’s attribution is substantial because the presidential system and presidential decrees shaped the institutional framework. Operational independence must be assessed separately.

**Limitations.** The source describes legal mandates rather than actual inspection staffing, enforcement, dissent, or corrective action.

**Contrary evidence.** NDK’s participation in MDEP and cooperation with international regulators suggests access to external expertise.

**Corroboration.** IAEA Integrated Regulatory Review Service materials; Turkish NDK annual reports; parliamentary and Constitutional Court records.

### WEB-1B-06 — Crisis diplomacy, military activity, and major-power exposure

**Claim.** In 2023 Türkiye condemned Russia’s aggression against Ukraine, facilitated Ukrainian grain exports and prisoner exchanges, and sought talks and de-escalation. It nevertheless did not align with EU sanctions, intensified economic ties with Russia, and continued military operations in Syria and Iraq.

**Why it matters.** Türkiye’s policy combined crisis-management diplomacy with conventional military activity and strategic dependence on a nuclear-armed Russia. This created both risk-reducing and risk-increasing elements in the country’s 2023 posture.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 7–8, foreign-policy and security sections: Ukraine, Grain Initiative, Russia, Syria, NATO, and dual-use trade.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. Foreign and security policy were highly centralized under the presidency, though ministries, armed forces, NATO, Russia, and coalition politics constrained implementation.

**Limitations.** The Commission evaluates Türkiye partly against EU foreign-policy preferences; its assessment should be paired with UN, NATO, Turkish, Russian, and Ukrainian records.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye’s mediation and grain diplomacy were concrete de-escalatory actions; it also banned certain sanctioned goods from transit to Russia.

**Corroboration.** UN Grain Initiative documents; NATO records; Turkish foreign-ministry statements; Carnegie and Crisis Group analyses.

### WEB-1B-07 — Cyber-risk policy framework

**Claim.** Türkiye had a National Cyber Security Strategy and Action Plan for 2020–2023, indicating a formal framework for managing cyber risk. However, implementation, resourcing, independent review, and 2023 outcomes were not established in this pass.

**Why it matters.** Cybersecurity is a catastrophic-risk and critical-infrastructure issue, particularly for nuclear, energy, defence, finance, health, and communications systems.

**Source.** *National Cyber Security Strategy and Action Plan 2020–2023*, Republic of Türkiye Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure.

**Locator.** Full document requires inspection; catalogue URL: https://hgm.uab.gov.tr/uploads/pages/siber-guvenlik/national-cyber-security-strategy-2020-2023.pdf

**Period fit.** Strategy covered the target year.

**Ruler attribution.** State-level attribution; Erdoğan’s role is indirect through executive control of cybersecurity and infrastructure policy.

**Limitations.** The document was not accessible during this pass, so this remains discovery/context evidence rather than final evidence.

**Contrary evidence.** None inspected.

**Corroboration.** BTK records, Cybersecurity Presidency materials, NATO cyber assessments, and 2023 critical-infrastructure incident reporting.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources attempted in this pass: 12.
- Sources substantively opened: 4.
- Accepted evidence sources: 4.
- Developed evidence records: 7.
- Major unresolved areas: nuclear rhetoric, actual Akkuyu inspection and emergency performance, NDK independence, export-control enforcement, cyber implementation, and the precise operational status and security arrangements at Incirlik.

The evidence supports a careful description of formal safeguards, expanding civilian nuclear capacity, NATO nuclear exposure, and mixed crisis diplomacy. It does not support a conclusion that Erdoğan pursued nuclear weapons, threatened nuclear use, or personally directed a nuclear-safety failure in 2023.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Türkiye had substantial international-security exposure in 2023:

- active Turkish military operations in northern Iraq and northern Syria;
- continuing PKK attacks and counterterrorism operations;
- support for or cooperation with Syrian armed actors;
- NATO obligations and disputes involving Sweden’s accession;
- mediation between Russia and Ukraine over grain exports and prisoner exchanges;
- tensions with Greece and Cyprus;
- regional refugee, migration, and border-security responsibilities.

Erdoğan was president and the dominant authority over foreign, defence, intelligence, and security policy. The presidential system concentrated executive authority, while parliament, NATO, courts, coalition partners, foreign governments, and operational commanders imposed practical limits. The European Commission described civilian oversight of security forces as incomplete and the executive as maintaining significant control.

The record is mixed. Türkiye pursued meaningful diplomacy over Ukraine and the grain deal and later submitted Sweden’s NATO accession protocol to parliament. At the same time, it continued cross-border operations in Iraq and Syria, expanded strikes after terrorist attacks, damaged civilian infrastructure according to U.S. and human-rights reporting, and faced allegations that its legal and public justifications were broader than the independently verified threat evidence. Türkiye’s security concerns were genuine, but the legality, proportionality, civilian protection, proxy control, and accountability of operations remain contested.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-01 | PKK attacks and Türkiye’s security response in Iraq and Syria | 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.8 |
| WEB-2B-02 | October 2023 escalation and strikes on northeastern Syria | 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-03 | December 2023 attacks on Turkish soldiers and retaliatory strikes | 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.4, 2B.8 |
| WEB-2B-04 | Damage to water, electricity, hospitals, factories, and other civilian infrastructure | 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.9, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-05 | Türkiye’s mediation and de-escalation role in the Russia–Ukraine war | 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-06 | NATO deconfliction crisis after a Turkish drone was shot down by U.S. forces | 2B.1, 2B.6, 2B.8, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-07 | Incomplete civilian oversight and weak legal accountability | 2B.6, 2B.9, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-08 | Counterterrorism legislation and international cooperation, alongside broad enforcement and due-process concerns | 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.9 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 2B.1 — Peaceful alternatives

**Disposition: mixed evidence.**

Türkiye used diplomacy in the Russia–Ukraine conflict, facilitated grain exports and prisoner exchanges, and sought ceasefire-oriented talks. The European Commission described these as meaningful diplomatic efforts.

For the PKK conflict, however, the inspected evidence shows no credible revival of a political peace process in 2023. The Commission reported that there was no progress toward resuming a credible political process on the Kurdish issue. Türkiye instead continued military operations in Iraq and Syria. Whether nonmilitary alternatives were realistically available requires deeper examination of the Kurdish political and security record.

Evidence: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-05.

### 2B.2 — Starting or prolonging war

**Disposition: significant adverse evidence, with defensive context.**

Türkiye conducted repeated airstrikes and other operations in northern Iraq and Syria. The United States assessed that Turkish attacks struck more than 150 locations in Syria in October and additional sites in December. Türkiye described the targets as PKK-linked and retaliatory or counterterrorist operations.

The PKK conducted attacks inside Türkiye and killed 12 Turkish soldiers in northern Iraq in December. These attacks provide a genuine security rationale, but the continuation and geographic scope of Turkish operations, especially against Syrian actors linked politically or organizationally to the PKK, require separate legality and proportionality analysis.

Evidence: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-03.

### 2B.3 — Honest justification for force

**Disposition: contested.**

Türkiye publicly characterized operations as targeting PKK forces and justified them under self-defence and counterterrorism. The U.S. Lead Inspector General report records Turkish claims and Turkish officials’ assertion that operations complied with the UN Charter and respected neighbours’ sovereignty.

However, it also reports that Türkiye did not distinguish between the PKK and the Syrian Democratic Forces (SDF), and that Turkish officials treated entities affiliated with the PKK, including civilian infrastructure, as valid targets. The breadth of those claims is difficult to reconcile with independent reporting on civilian harm and infrastructure damage.

Evidence: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-03.

### 2B.4 — Civilian and prisoner protection

**Disposition: adverse evidence requiring further legal verification.**

The U.S. Lead Inspector General report records reports that Turkish strikes damaged water and electricity systems, hospitals, factories, and other civilian infrastructure. The U.S. State Department’s terrorism report stated that October strikes in northeast Syria killed 10 civilians, injured others, and caused significant infrastructure damage.

The inspected materials do not establish whether Turkish rules of engagement were formally lawful, whether precautions were taken in each incident, or whether violations were investigated and remedied.

Evidence: WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-04.

### 2B.5 — Control of proxies and arms

**Disposition: unresolved, with a major research gap.**

Türkiye’s relationship with Syrian armed groups and the SDF/YPG is central to this lens. The inspected sources establish that Türkiye treated the SDF and YPG as connected to the PKK, while the United States partnered with the SDF against ISIS. They do not adequately establish Türkiye’s command, financing, weapons-transfer, monitoring, or disciplinary controls over Turkish-backed Syrian forces.

Further research should use UN commissions, Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, U.S. oversight records, and Turkish operational documents.

Evidence: contextual support from WEB-2B-02; no complete accepted record yet.

### 2B.6 — Scrutiny of war claims

**Disposition: adverse institutional evidence.**

The European Commission found incomplete civilian oversight of security forces and a continuing need to strengthen parliamentary oversight. It also reported pressure on journalists, civil society, and critical voices. The U.S. Lead Inspector General report provides an important external check by recording Turkish claims alongside U.S., Kurdish, Iraqi, human-rights, and media accounts.

Domestic scrutiny was therefore constrained, while external scrutiny remained possible but incomplete.

Evidence: WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-06, WEB-2B-07, WEB-2B-08.

### 2B.7 — Ceasefires and settlements

**Disposition: partial positive evidence; weak durable-settlement record.**

Türkiye participated in the UN–Türkiye Black Sea Grain Initiative, facilitated prisoner exchanges, and sought talks between Russia and Ukraine. These were concrete conflict-management efforts, although Russia terminated the grain arrangement in July 2023.

There was no comparable progress in a durable political settlement of the Kurdish conflict. The European Commission specifically reported no credible restart of the peace process.

Evidence: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-05.

### 2B.8 — Responsible military resources

**Disposition: unresolved and mixed.**

Türkiye maintained significant military capabilities and conducted extensive cross-border operations. The inspected sources show operational scale and consequences but do not provide adequate 2023 budget, procurement, audit, or patronage evidence.

The military response was connected to genuine PKK attacks, but the proportionality of repeated strikes, the use of drones, and the strategic purpose of maintaining bases and operations in Iraq and Syria require further investigation.

Evidence: WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-03; budget and procurement gap.

### 2B.9 — Accountability and remedy

**Disposition: weak evidence of effective remedy.**

The European Commission found incomplete security-force oversight, weak judicial independence, and continued concerns over implementation of European Court of Human Rights judgments. The U.S. and State Department reports document civilian-harm allegations but do not show that Türkiye conducted transparent, independent investigations or provided meaningful remedy.

Evidence: WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-07, WEB-2B-08.

### 2B.10 — Lasting international peace

**Disposition: unresolved and mixed.**

Türkiye left 2023 with a significant diplomatic role in Ukraine-related mediation and NATO bargaining, but also with continuing military operations, unresolved Kurdish conflict, strained relations with Iraq and Syria, and recurring risks of confrontation with U.S. forces and other NATO members.

The available evidence does not establish a durable improvement in regional peace or lawful security governance.

Evidence: WEB-2B-01 through WEB-2B-08.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-2B-01 — PKK attacks and Türkiye’s cross-border security operations

**Claim and significance.** The PKK conducted attacks in Türkiye and against Turkish interests abroad during 2023. Türkiye responded with domestic operations and military action in northern Iraq and northern Syria. The U.S. State Department reported that Türkiye conducted airstrikes following the 1 October Ankara attack and the 22–23 December attacks that killed 12 Turkish soldiers.

This establishes the conflict-exposure gate and a genuine defensive-security context, while leaving open questions about necessity, proportionality, sovereignty, and alternatives.

**Source.** *Country Report on Terrorism 2023 — Chapter 1: Türkiye*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net, 2024.

**Locator.** “Overview” and “2023 Terrorist Incidents,” especially lines 5–10.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for strategic authorization and public policy; operational responsibility was shared with the armed forces, intelligence services, and ministries.

**Limitations.** The report relies partly on government and open-source information and uses the U.S. designation of the PKK. It is not an independent judicial determination of every operation’s legality.

**Contrary evidence.** The report also records civilian deaths and infrastructure damage from Turkish airstrikes.

**Corroboration.** European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*; U.S. Lead Inspector General report; ACLED; UCDP; Turkish official statements.

### WEB-2B-02 — October escalation and damage in northeastern Syria

**Claim and significance.** Following the 1 October PKK-claimed attack in Ankara, Türkiye launched days of strikes in Iraq and Syria. The U.S. Lead Inspector General reported that Türkiye struck more than 150 locations in Syria between 5 and 10 October, causing deaths, injuries, and damage to critical infrastructure. Human Rights Watch reported disruption to water and electricity affecting millions.

This is the strongest inspected evidence of possible civilian-harm and proportionality concerns in the target period.

**Source.** *Operation Inherent Resolve Quarterly Report to Congress, October 1, 2023–December 31, 2023*, U.S. Department of Defense Lead Inspector General, February 2024.

**Locator.** pp. 42–46, especially lines 1738–1806.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence, published shortly afterward.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong at the strategic level: the operation followed an attack and was publicly backed by the Turkish government. Individual strike attribution and targeting decisions require operational records.

**Limitations.** The report compiles U.S. government, Turkish, SDF, media, and NGO reporting; some casualty claims are attributed rather than independently verified.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye stated that targets were PKK-linked and that operations complied with the UN Charter. The PKK and SDF also conducted attacks and disputed Turkish claims.

**Corroboration.** Human Rights Watch; U.S. State Department terrorism report; SDF statements; Turkish Defence Ministry releases.

### WEB-2B-03 — December retaliation after 12 Turkish soldiers were killed

**Claim and significance.** PKK attacks in northern Iraq on 22–23 December killed 12 Turkish soldiers. Türkiye responded with strikes on 29 targets, according to reporting summarized by the U.S. Lead Inspector General, and announced or conducted further attacks in Iraq and Syria.

The episode is important for assessing whether force was defensive retaliation, disproportionate escalation, or part of a broader ongoing campaign.

**Source.** *Operation Inherent Resolve Quarterly Report to Congress, October 1, 2023–December 31, 2023*, U.S. Department of Defense Lead Inspector General.

**Locator.** pp. 44–46, lines 1830–1850 and 4934–4959.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national response and strategic direction; individual targeting remains operationally distributed.

**Limitations.** Casualty and target figures are reported through media and government sources. The source records reports of civilian deaths but does not adjudicate them.

**Contrary evidence.** The PKK attacks provide a direct security trigger and were among the deadliest attacks on Turkish forces in northern Iraq in several years.

**Corroboration.** U.S. State Department terrorism report; Reuters and Associated Press reporting; Iraqi government and Kurdistan Regional Government statements.

### WEB-2B-04 — Civilian infrastructure and civilian-harm allegations

**Claim and significance.** Turkish strikes in northeastern Syria reportedly damaged or disrupted water, electricity, hospitals, factories, and other infrastructure. The U.S. State Department reported 10 civilian deaths and additional injuries from October strikes; the Lead Inspector General report records further allegations of civilian deaths and infrastructure harm.

These claims directly concern civilian protection, foreseeable consequences, and remedy.

**Source.** *Operation Inherent Resolve Quarterly Report to Congress*, U.S. Department of Defense Lead Inspector General; *Country Report on Terrorism 2023 — Türkiye*, U.S. Department of State.

**Locator.** Lead Inspector General report pp. 42–46; State Department report, overview, lines 5–7.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong at policy level; incident-level attribution requires strike logs and independent investigations.

**Limitations.** Several figures derive from rights groups, war monitors, SDF statements, or media accounts. Some events occurred in areas where independent access was limited.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye described the targets as terrorist infrastructure and did not accept the characterization of its operations as unlawful attacks on civilians.

**Corroboration.** Human Rights Watch; U.S. State Department; SDF and local administrative statements; satellite and infrastructure assessments.

### WEB-2B-05 — Grain diplomacy and Russia–Ukraine mediation

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye condemned Russia’s aggression against Ukraine, helped facilitate Ukrainian grain exports and prisoner exchanges, and sought talks and de-escalation. The UN–Türkiye Grain Initiative ended when Russia withdrew in July 2023.

This is concrete evidence that Erdoğan’s government used diplomatic authority to reduce humanitarian and international-security risks, although it did not achieve a durable settlement.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 7–8, lines 296–309.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong: Erdoğan personally and institutionally directed Türkiye’s high-level foreign policy and mediation.

**Limitations.** The European Commission evaluates some policies against EU positions and does not provide the full negotiation record.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye did not align with EU restrictive measures against Russia and increased economic dependence on Russia, complicating its peace-making role.

**Corroboration.** UN Grain Initiative records; Turkish Foreign Ministry; Russian and Ukrainian statements; Carnegie and Crisis Group analyses.

### WEB-2B-06 — U.S. shooting down of a Turkish drone in Syria

**Claim and significance.** On 5 October 2023, during the Turkish bombardment in Syria, U.S. forces shot down a Turkish unmanned aircraft after assessing it as a potential threat inside a U.S.-restricted operating zone. U.S. officials then emphasized communication and deconfliction to prevent further incidents.

This event demonstrates the escalation risk created by overlapping Turkish, U.S., SDF, and anti-ISIS operations, while also showing crisis-management efforts after the incident.

**Source.** *Operation Inherent Resolve Quarterly Report to Congress*, U.S. Department of Defense Lead Inspector General.

**Locator.** pp. 45–46, lines 1800–1829.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The drone operated under Turkish authority, but the precise chain of command and mission authorization is not in the public record.

**Limitations.** U.S. operational reporting naturally reflects U.S. force-protection priorities and does not provide the Turkish account in full.

**Contrary evidence.** The U.S. called the action “regrettable” but “prudent”; subsequent bilateral meetings sought to prevent recurrence.

**Corroboration.** USCENTCOM statement; Turkish Defence Ministry; NATO and congressional records.

### WEB-2B-07 — Incomplete civilian oversight of security forces

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission found that civilian oversight of Türkiye’s security forces remained incomplete, that the executive retained significant control, and that parliamentary oversight and accountability mechanisms were inadequate.

This weakens confidence that military conduct, casualty claims, and security justifications received effective domestic scrutiny.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 4–6, especially lines 170–176 and 186–197.

**Period fit.** Reporting period through June 2023, with relevance to the remainder of 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution to the presidential executive, while not proving personal responsibility for each oversight failure.

**Limitations.** The report is an external assessment and does not replace Turkish parliamentary or judicial records.

**Contrary evidence.** Formal civilian courts and parliamentary institutions continued to exist, and international oversight remained possible.

**Corroboration.** Venice Commission; European Parliament; Turkish Grand National Assembly; ECtHR records.

### WEB-2B-08 — Counterterrorism capacity alongside broad enforcement and due-process concerns

**Claim and significance.** The U.S. State Department reported Turkish counterterrorism cooperation, arrests of ISIS suspects, financial-intelligence reforms, and international engagement. It simultaneously described Türkiye’s terrorism definition as broad and reported use of counterterrorism laws against expression and peaceful assembly, as well as detention based on scant evidence and limited due process in some FETÖ-related cases.

This shows a dual record: real security capacity and cooperation, but legal overreach that can undermine lawful security.

**Source.** *Country Report on Terrorism 2023 — Chapter 1: Türkiye*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** Lines 11–18.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national counterterrorism policy and executive institutions; case-level responsibility remains distributed among police, prosecutors, courts, and ministries.

**Limitations.** The report relies partly on Turkish government claims and does not resolve the merits of individual prosecutions.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye conducted real operations against ISIS and improved some financial-intelligence and interagency practices.

**Corroboration.** FATF; European Commission; Turkish Ministry of Interior; ECtHR and Constitutional Court judgments.

## 5. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*.
2. U.S. Department of Defense Lead Inspector General, *Operation Inherent Resolve Quarterly Report*, October–December 2023.
3. U.S. Department of State, *Country Report on Terrorism 2023 — Türkiye*.
4. U.S. Department of Defense and State reporting as reproduced by ecoi.net.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- ACLED, *Türkiye’s 2023 General Elections and the Kurdish Question* — page accessible only as a holding/interstitial page.
- European External Action Service Türkiye country archive.
- International Crisis Group Türkiye archive.
- Carnegie Endowment Türkiye research archive.
- Anadolu Agency political archive.
- Al Jazeera Erdoğan archive.
- Associated Press candidate records on grain diplomacy, NATO, and Kurdish operations.

### Uninspected or still needing extraction

- UCDP and PA-X conflict-event records.
- UN OHCHR report on civilian deaths in Syria.
- Human Rights Watch report on Turkish strikes disrupting water and electricity.
- Amnesty International reporting on Syrian armed opposition groups.
- Iraqi government and Kurdistan Regional Government protests concerning sovereignty and civilian harm.
- Turkish parliamentary transcript on military operations and expenditure.
- NATO and Turkish records on Sweden accession and deconfliction.
- U.S. Department of State and Turkish Ministry of Defence primary statements.
- Full ACLED analysis of the Kurdish question.
- Turkish-backed proxy command, arms-transfer, and disciplinary records.

### Blocked or technically inaccessible

- ACLED report returned an access/interstitial page.
- UN Digital Library OHCHR PDF could not be substantively opened.
- Associated Press, Carnegie, Crisis Group, EEAS, and several archive pages returned tool errors or incomplete content.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources attempted: 12.
- Sources substantively opened: 4 primary or institutional records, with several catalogue pages partially accessible.
- Accepted evidence records: 8.
- Principal unresolved issues: proxy-force control, Turkish rules of engagement, independent casualty verification, Iraqi sovereignty and consent, military budgets and procurement, domestic investigations, and the availability of nonmilitary alternatives to cross-border operations.

No score is assigned.
