--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

The physical-safety record for 2023 was shaped by the February earthquakes, emergency policing, detention and torture allegations, protest policing, counterterrorism enforcement, violence against women, and risks faced by refugees and other vulnerable groups.

Erdoğan was president and controlled the executive, police, gendarmerie, intelligence services, emergency administration, and national security policy. Formal limits included parliament, courts, prosecutors, the Constitutional Court, the Ombudsman, local authorities, and international human-rights bodies. In practice, the European Commission found that the executive retained significant control over security forces, while civilian oversight and judicial independence remained incomplete.

The most serious target-year evidence concerns alleged police, gendarmerie, and military abuse in earthquake-affected provinces. Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International documented 13 cases involving 34 victims, including beatings, arbitrary detention, threats, and alleged torture. Amnesty reported that at least one person died in custody and that three gendarmes were suspended.

The record also contains positive elements: Türkiye maintained emergency hotlines and protection institutions for domestic violence, conducted counterterrorism operations against ISIS and the PKK, and provided some earthquake relief and refugee assistance. However, protection was undermined by weak accountability, restrictions on reporting, broad counterterrorism laws, ineffective enforcement of restraining orders, and discriminatory treatment of refugees and minorities.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-3B-01 | Earthquake-zone police, gendarmerie, and military abuse | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.5, 3B.6, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-02 | At least one death in custody and limited accountability response | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-03 | Emergency powers and restrictions on earthquake reporting | 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.8, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-04 | Abuse and discriminatory treatment of Syrian refugees after the earthquakes | 3B.3, 3B.7, 3B.9, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-05 | Continuing torture, detention, prison, and disappearance concerns | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5 |
| WEB-3B-06 | Protest policing and unlawful force | 3B.4, 3B.8 |
| WEB-3B-07 | Gender-based violence and weak enforcement of protection measures | 3B.3, 3B.4, 3B.7, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-08 | Refugee and disability protection gaps after the earthquake | 3B.7, 3B.9, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-09 | Security-force oversight and judicial-accountability weakness | 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.6 |
| WEB-3B-10 | Counterterrorism capacity alongside broad legal enforcement | 3B.1, 3B.5, 3B.6 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 3B.1 — Protection from state abuse

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

Human Rights Watch and Amnesty documented torture and other ill-treatment by police, gendarmerie, and soldiers in the earthquake zone. The U.S. State Department also reported allegations involving police, prison authorities, military personnel, and intelligence units, including abuse of people suspected of PKK or Gülen affiliation.

The evidence establishes serious incidents and recurring allegations, but not the full national prevalence of torture or a documented Erdoğan order authorizing abuse.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-05.

### 3B.2 — Control of coercive forces

**Disposition: adverse evidence concerning prevention and discipline.**

The executive maintained significant control over security forces, but the European Commission found civilian oversight incomplete. In earthquake-zone cases, officers allegedly participated in beatings, failed to intervene, or transported victims to unofficial detention sites. Amnesty reported that three gendarmes were suspended after a death in custody, but broader accountability remains unclear.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-09.

### 3B.3 — Incitement and targeted hatred

**Disposition: mixed, with evidence of discriminatory official and social environments.**

Human Rights Watch and Amnesty documented racist abuse of Syrian refugees after the earthquakes and anti-refugee rhetoric during the election period. The European Commission reported hate speech and discrimination against minorities and LGBTIQ people.

The inspected evidence does not establish that Erdoğan personally ordered physical violence or directly incited a specific 2023 attack. It does establish a political environment in which vulnerable groups faced heightened exposure and insufficient protection.

Evidence: WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-07, WEB-3B-08.

### 3B.4 — Complaints and independent oversight

**Disposition: weak and adverse.**

The European Commission found weak civilian oversight, executive pressure on judges and prosecutors, incomplete implementation of ECtHR judgments, and inadequate accountability mechanisms. Victims of earthquake-zone abuse filed complaints, but the inspected record does not show prompt, independent, comprehensive investigations.

Domestic-violence complaint systems existed, including hotlines and emergency applications, but the U.S. State Department reported that police often failed to enforce restraining orders effectively.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-07, WEB-3B-09.

### 3B.5 — Emergency and surveillance powers

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

Erdoğan declared a state of emergency in the ten earthquake-affected provinces on 7 February 2023; parliament approved it two days later. The emergency enabled broad control over resources, movement, businesses, and relief operations.

Amnesty and the U.S. State Department reported restrictions on Twitter and TikTok during rescue operations and detention of at least 257 people for criticizing the earthquake response. The state argued that content controls were necessary under national-security laws, but critics viewed them as limiting scrutiny of government failures.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-05.

### 3B.6 — Safety without state terror

**Disposition: mixed but adverse on state-abuse risk.**

Türkiye faced genuine earthquake, terrorism, looting, and public-order threats. It conducted counterterrorism operations and maintained law-enforcement capacity. However, documented beatings, arbitrary detention, threats, and alleged torture during the earthquake emergency show that emergency security measures themselves exposed civilians to serious harm.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-10.

### 3B.7 — Protection of vulnerable groups

**Disposition: materially inadequate.**

Evidence indicates heightened risks for Syrian refugees, women, children, persons with disabilities, Roma communities, and LGBTIQ people. Refugees experienced physical abuse, racist harassment, displacement from emergency camps, and barriers to movement. Earthquake damage reduced health and reproductive-health access, especially for refugee women and girls and people in rural areas. Women’s organizations reported inadequate shelters and weak police enforcement of protection orders.

Evidence: WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-07, WEB-3B-08.

### 3B.8 — Safe protest and dissent

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

The European Commission reported recurrent bans, disproportionate force, investigations, prosecutions, and administrative fines connected to demonstrations. Amnesty reported unlawful force amounting to torture or other ill-treatment during the detention of at least five people at the Istanbul Trans Pride March in June 2023.

Evidence: WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-06, WEB-3B-09.

### 3B.9 — Restrained crisis response

**Disposition: mixed, with serious failures.**

The earthquake response included large-scale state and international relief efforts, but emergency governance also involved restrictions on movement and reporting, alleged torture and unofficial detention, discriminatory treatment of refugees, and inadequate access for disabled people and women.

The central unresolved issue is whether these were isolated misconducts or foreseeable products of emergency policy, command culture, and weak accountability.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-08.

### 3B.10 — Lasting domestic safety

**Disposition: unresolved, with substantial adverse evidence.**

Türkiye retained meaningful capacity against terrorism and organized crime and operated formal domestic-violence reporting mechanisms. But 2023 ended with unresolved torture allegations, weak oversight, protest-policing concerns, discriminatory exposure of refugees, and persistent gender-based violence.

The available evidence does not establish that people were durably safer from both state and non-state violence than before the period.

Evidence: WEB-3B-01 through WEB-3B-10.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-3B-01 — Earthquake-zone abuse by police, gendarmerie, and military personnel

**Claim and significance.** Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International investigated 13 cases involving 34 victims and reported beatings, threats, arbitrary detention, and other ill-treatment by police, gendarmes, and soldiers in earthquake-affected provinces. Most cases occurred in Antakya and involved people suspected of looting; four involved Syrian refugees.

**Source.** *Turkey: Police and Gendarmerie Abuses in Earthquake Zone*, Human Rights Watch, 5 April 2023.

**Locator.** “Torture and Other Ill-Treatment” and case summaries; especially lines 601–607 and 647–680.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong. Erdoğan declared the state of emergency and controlled the executive security apparatus. Individual assaults require incident-level attribution; the evidence does not prove a presidential order.

**Limitations.** The organizations fully corroborated 13 cases but could not verify all additional allegations. The government rejected the findings as vague, without addressing the specific cases.

**Contrary evidence.** Authorities formally maintained a “zero tolerance for torture” policy and some victims filed complaints.

**Corroboration.** Amnesty’s 2023 human-rights report; U.S. State Department human-rights report; Turkish medical and prosecutor records.

### WEB-3B-02 — Death in custody and limited disciplinary response

**Claim and significance.** Amnesty reported that at least one person detained for alleged looting after the earthquakes died in custody after torture or other ill-treatment. Three gendarmes were suspended on 15 February.

This is important because it connects emergency policing, lethal physical harm, and the question of whether discipline was prompt and systemic or merely limited.

**Source.** *The State of the World’s Human Rights; Türkiye 2023*, Amnesty International, 2024, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** “Torture and other ill-treatment,” lines 71–74.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence, later annual retrospective.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The incident involved local security personnel, while national attribution concerns command responsibility, emergency policy, and remedy.

**Limitations.** The accessible annual-report passage provides limited case detail and does not identify the victim or final judicial outcome.

**Contrary evidence.** Suspension of three gendarmes indicates at least some disciplinary response.

**Corroboration.** Human Rights Watch and Amnesty joint earthquake-zone investigation; prosecutor and court records remain needed.

### WEB-3B-03 — Emergency powers and restrictions on earthquake reporting

**Claim and significance.** The state of emergency was declared by Erdoğan on 7 February and approved by parliament two days later. It authorized extensive executive control over resources and movement. The U.S. State Department reported that internet access to Twitter was throttled by approximately 90 percent on 8 February; Amnesty reported that at least 257 people were detained for criticizing the earthquake response.

This matters because restricting information during a disaster can obstruct rescue coordination, expose abuse, and prevent public oversight.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Turkey*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net; and Amnesty International’s 2023 report.

**Locator.** State Department lines 120 and 191; Amnesty report lines 14–16.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for declaration and national emergency policy; internet enforcement and individual detentions were carried out by subordinate institutions.

**Limitations.** The bandwidth estimate and detention total depend on reporting by rights organizations and government-related sources; the precise legal basis for each detention requires case records.

**Contrary evidence.** The government argued that restrictions addressed unlawful or national-security-threatening content during a chaotic disaster.

**Corroboration.** Constitutional Court decisions, BTK records, parliamentary debate, journalist cases, and AFAD communications records.

### WEB-3B-04 — Abuse and discriminatory treatment of Syrian refugees

**Claim and significance.** Amnesty reported that Syrian refugees experienced physical abuse and racist harassment after the earthquakes, including abuse by civilians and state actors. Some were evicted from emergency camps to make room for Turkish survivors. Human Rights Watch documented abusive and humiliating treatment of Syrian refugees by police, gendarmes, and soldiers.

**Source.** *The State of the World’s Human Rights; Türkiye 2023*, Amnesty International; and Human Rights Watch earthquake-zone investigation.

**Locator.** Amnesty lines 58–62; Human Rights Watch lines 678–680.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The incidents were decentralized, but national authorities controlled emergency rules, refugee administration, police deployment, and anti-discrimination enforcement.

**Limitations.** Some reports are based on interviews and rights investigations in a chaotic emergency zone. Broader prevalence is uncertain.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye hosted a very large refugee population and international aid supported refugees and host communities.

**Corroboration.** UNHCR protection assessment; European Commission report; Turkish migration authorities; local NGO records.

### WEB-3B-05 — Torture, detention, and protest-policing concerns

**Claim and significance.** Amnesty reported that people detained for alleged earthquake looting were tortured or otherwise ill-treated and that, during the June Trans Pride March, police used unlawful force amounting to torture or other ill-treatment against at least five protesters.

The incidents show that coercive practices extended beyond the earthquake response and affected both suspected crime and peaceful dissent.

**Source.** *The State of the World’s Human Rights; Türkiye 2023*, Amnesty International.

**Locator.** “Torture and other ill-treatment,” lines 71–74; protest-related passage immediately following.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for national policing and assembly policy; individual force decisions require local records.

**Limitations.** Amnesty is an advocacy organization and its annual report summarizes selected incidents rather than providing a complete national dataset.

**Contrary evidence.** Authorities retained formal legal prohibitions on torture and some cases were investigated or disciplined.

**Corroboration.** European Commission; Human Rights Watch; U.S. State Department; Constitutional Court and ECtHR records.

### WEB-3B-06 — Restrictions and force against peaceful demonstrations

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported recurrent bans, disproportionate force, investigations, prosecutions, and fines involving demonstrations and assemblies. This indicates that protest policing created physical-safety risks, not merely speech restrictions.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 5–7, especially lines 245–250.

**Period fit.** Reporting period through June 2023, with relevance to 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. National legislation and executive policy shaped the framework, but municipal and local police actions require specific attribution.

**Limitations.** The Commission aggregates incidents and does not provide a complete force-use dataset.

**Contrary evidence.** Peaceful civic activity continued, and opposition parties and civil society remained active.

**Corroboration.** Amnesty; Freedom House; Turkish Medical Association and bar-association records; court judgments.

### WEB-3B-07 — Gender-based violence and ineffective protective enforcement

**Claim and significance.** The U.S. State Department reported that gender-based violence remained widespread; police often failed to enforce restraining orders effectively; some officials encouraged women to remain in abusive marriages; shelters were insufficient; and courts sometimes reduced sentences using “unjustifiable provocation” or good-conduct reasoning. As of September, NGOs reported that men had killed 247 women.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Turkey*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** Lines 326–332.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Domestic violence is primarily perpetrated by private actors, but enforcement, shelters, police practice, courts, and national policy are state responsibilities.

**Limitations.** The homicide count is NGO-reported and the report does not establish a single national administrative series.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye operated a nationwide hotline and Women Emergency Assistance Notification System; courts regularly issued restraining orders.

**Corroboration.** UNFPA–TKDF domestic-violence report; Turkish Women’s Associations Federation data; Ministry of Family and Social Services records.

### WEB-3B-08 — Disability, refugee, and health-protection gaps after the earthquakes

**Claim and significance.** The U.S. State Department reported that the earthquakes affected approximately 1.7 million refugees, that some faced travel-permit problems, and that reproductive-health and other medical services deteriorated. Amnesty reported that food, water, and aid distribution did not adequately account for persons with disabilities and that many injured people were expected to live with disabilities.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Turkey*, U.S. Department of State; *The State of the World’s Human Rights; Türkiye 2023*, Amnesty International.

**Locator.** State Department lines 221 and 338–340; Amnesty lines 47–60.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The earthquake was an external shock, but equitable emergency allocation, health access, refugee movement, and disability protection were governmental responsibilities.

**Limitations.** Damage and service-access estimates changed over time; municipal, national, and international providers shared responsibility.

**Contrary evidence.** The government and international donors delivered large-scale emergency assistance and permitted some refugee travel exemptions.

**Corroboration.** UNICEF, UNHCR, WHO, World Bank, AFAD, and local disability organizations.

### WEB-3B-09 — Weak civilian oversight and judicial remedy

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission found that civilian oversight of security forces remained incomplete, the executive retained significant control, parliamentary oversight was weak, and judicial independence suffered from executive pressure. It also cited failure to implement certain ECtHR judgments.

This is central to whether abuse could be independently reported, investigated, punished, and remedied.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 4–6, lines 170–176, 186–197, and 223–236.

**Period fit.** Reporting period through June 2023; structural relevance throughout 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution to the presidential executive, though not proof of personal involvement in every case.

**Limitations.** It is an external institutional assessment and should be paired with domestic judgments and case files.

**Contrary evidence.** Formal courts, prosecutors, parliament, and complaint institutions continued to exist.

**Corroboration.** ECtHR judgments; Constitutional Court; Turkish Ombudsman; Human Rights and Equality Institution of Türkiye.

### WEB-3B-10 — Counterterrorism capacity alongside broad enforcement

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye maintained real counterterrorism capacity, including operations against ISIS, PKK-linked groups, and terrorist-financing networks. At the same time, the U.S. State Department reported that broad counterterrorism laws were used against people exercising expression and peaceful assembly, while some terrorism-related detainees experienced scant evidence and limited due process.

**Source.** *Country Report on Terrorism 2023 — Chapter 1: Türkiye*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** Lines 5–18.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national counterterrorism policy; case-level attribution is distributed among police, intelligence, prosecutors, and courts.

**Limitations.** The report includes Turkish government claims and does not adjudicate individual cases.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye conducted arrests and prevention programs against ISIS and improved some financial-intelligence procedures.

**Corroboration.** FATF; European Commission; Turkish Ministry of Interior; ECtHR and Constitutional Court records.

## 5. Information environment and evidentiary limits

Evidence was available from international organizations, courts, human-rights NGOs, government reports, and survivor interviews, but several conditions made verification difficult:

- restrictions on social-media access and earthquake reporting;
- detention and prosecution of critics;
- fear of retaliation against victims, lawyers, journalists, and witnesses;
- limited access to emergency zones;
- government rejection of NGO findings without full case-by-case responses;
- polarized claims about looting, refugees, disaster response, and security;
- incomplete police, prison, and judicial transparency;
- underreporting of domestic violence and gender-based killings;
- difficulty separating national policy from municipal policing or individual misconduct.

The strongest evidence for earthquake-zone abuse combines survivor interviews, medical records, complaints, video verification, and corroborating journalism. The strongest evidence for institutional conditions comes from the European Commission and U.S. State Department. The domestic-violence evidence is less complete and relies substantially on NGO reporting and hotline data.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Political Freedom vs. Authoritarian Rule — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Erdoğan was president throughout 2023 and leader of the AK Parti. The presidential system concentrated executive authority in the presidency, including influence over ministries, regulators, appointments, public broadcasting, security institutions, and much of the administrative state. Parliament remained competitive and the May elections were genuinely contested, but the ruling coalition retained a majority and the incumbent enjoyed major institutional and media advantages.

The 2023 political record is therefore mixed in form but adverse in substance:

- voters had meaningful alternatives and turnout was high;
- Erdoğan accepted the election result and began a new term through constitutional procedures;
- the electoral field was not level;
- public broadcasters favored the ruling coalition;
- opposition parties and candidates faced prosecutions, intimidation, closure proceedings, and unequal access;
- courts, the Supreme Electoral Council, regulators, and local governments lacked sufficient independence;
- the 2022 Disinformation Law and other criminal provisions chilled online and traditional speech;
- political participation by women and some minorities remained unequal.

The strongest source is the OSCE/ODIHR final election report, which directly observed the 14 May parliamentary and first-round presidential elections and the 28 May runoff. It concluded that voters had genuine alternatives and high participation, but the incumbent and ruling parties enjoyed an unjustified advantage. [OSCE/ODIHR, *Türkiye 2023 Election Observation Mission Final Report*](https://odihr.osce.org/sites/default/files/f/documents/1/f/553966.pdf)

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-4B-01 | Competitive elections with an unjustified incumbent advantage | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.8, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-02 | Erdoğan’s early-election decision and disputed eligibility | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-03 | Pressure on HDP/YSP and opposition politicians | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.7 |
| WEB-4B-04 | Misuse of administrative resources and campaign finance weaknesses | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.5 |
| WEB-4B-05 | Public-media bias and pressure on independent media | 4B.3, 4B.5, 4B.6 |
| WEB-4B-06 | Disinformation Law, online censorship, and criminalization of speech | 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9 |
| WEB-4B-07 | Weak election-administration transparency and ineffective remedies | 4B.4, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-08 | Unequal political representation of women and minorities | 4B.7, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-09 | Centralized executive authority and weak checks and balances | 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-10 | Judicial and civic-space constraints | 4B.3, 4B.4, 4B.5 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 4B.1 — Genuine electoral choice

**Disposition: mixed but materially constrained.**

The May elections were competitive, high-turnout contests with genuine alternatives. Erdoğan required a second presidential round and ultimately won through the formal electoral process.

However, OSCE/ODIHR found biased media coverage, misuse of administrative resources, restrictions on opposition participation, unequal campaign conditions, and deficiencies in election administration and dispute resolution. The result was electoral competition without a genuinely level field.

Evidence: WEB-4B-01, WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-04, WEB-4B-05.

### 4B.2 — No entrenchment of power

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

The presidential system centralized authority, and the incumbent used official functions and state-linked resources during the campaign. Erdoğan announced moving the election from 18 June to 14 May, and the Supreme Electoral Council rejected challenges to his eligibility for another term.

The inspected evidence does not establish that the election date or eligibility ruling was unlawful, but both were politically consequential and contested.

Evidence: WEB-4B-02, WEB-4B-04, WEB-4B-09.

### 4B.3 — Opposition and civic freedom

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

OSCE/ODIHR reported pressure, intimidation, and arrests affecting HDP/YSP supporters, ongoing closure proceedings against the HDP, prosecution of opposition figures, restrictions on civil society, and continuing limitations on assembly, association, and expression.

Opposition parties remained active and were able to campaign, but the risks and costs of opposition were materially greater than for the incumbent.

Evidence: WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-06, WEB-4B-10.

### 4B.4 — Independent checks on power

**Disposition: weak and adverse.**

The European Commission found that executive checks and balances were ineffective, regulatory bodies were closely linked to the presidency, judicial independence had deteriorated, and parliamentary oversight of security and administration remained insufficient.

OSCE/ODIHR also found that the Supreme Electoral Council and lower electoral bodies operated technically efficiently but with inadequate transparency and limited public confidence in independence.

Evidence: WEB-4B-07, WEB-4B-09, WEB-4B-10.

### 4B.5 — Politically neutral institutions

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

The European Commission reported centralized policymaking, politicization of public administration, and insufficient accountability. OSCE/ODIHR found that Erdoğan campaigned during official duties, inaugurated infrastructure during the campaign, and announced social benefits while campaigning, blurring the line between party and state.

Evidence: WEB-4B-04, WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-09.

### 4B.6 — Independent information and media

**Disposition: strongly adverse.**

OSCE/ODIHR found that public broadcasters clearly favored ruling parties and candidates. Freedom House reported that most major media were owned by businesses dependent on public tenders or close to Erdoğan, while independent outlets faced pressure, prosecution, fines, and closure.

The election-period media environment reduced voters’ ability to make an informed choice.

Evidence: WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-06.

### 4B.7 — Equal political rights

**Disposition: materially unequal.**

Women were constitutionally equal but underrepresented: OSCE/ODIHR reported that women comprised only about one quarter of parliamentary candidates, no woman ran for president, and women held limited shares of ministerial and local-government positions. HDP/YSP supporters and Kurdish political actors faced distinctive pressure and arrests. Campaign rhetoric also included discriminatory remarks about migrants, refugees, and LGBTI people.

Evidence: WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-08.

### 4B.8 — Term limits and transfer

**Disposition: procedurally compliant but contested.**

Erdoğan’s continuation followed an election and runoff under the existing presidential system. However, opposition actors challenged his eligibility for another term, arguing that it could constitute an unconstitutional third term. The Supreme Electoral Council dismissed those challenges.

The evidence supports a formal transfer/continuation process, but the underlying constitutional interpretation remains an important legal issue.

Evidence: WEB-4B-02.

### 4B.9 — Digital freedom and surveillance

**Disposition: strongly adverse.**

Freedom House reported that the Disinformation Law criminalized vaguely defined false online information with prison terms of one to three years, and that authorities removed content, blocked websites, and prosecuted users during the election period. Social-media platforms were restricted after the February earthquakes, and opposition websites were attacked during the election period.

Evidence: WEB-4B-06.

### 4B.10 — Lasting democratic resilience

**Disposition: adverse and unresolved.**

Türkiye retained competitive elections, high participation, opposition parties, and formal constitutional procedures. But the institutional environment remained highly centralized, media pluralism was weak, judicial and electoral remedies lacked public confidence, and opposition participation faced legal and administrative pressure.

The available evidence does not show that political freedom or democratic resilience was durably strengthened during 2023.

Evidence: WEB-4B-01 through WEB-4B-10.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-4B-01 — Competitive elections with an unjustified incumbent advantage

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR concluded that voters had genuine political alternatives and participation was high, but Erdoğan and the ruling parties enjoyed an unjustified advantage through biased media coverage, restrictions on fundamental freedoms, misuse of administrative resources, and pressure on an opposition party.

**Source.** *Republic of Türkiye: General Elections 14 May and Presidential Election, Second Round 28 May 2023 — Final Report*, OSCE/ODIHR, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Executive Summary, pp. 1–4, especially lines 53–73.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for Erdoğan’s campaign advantage and use of presidential office; broader electoral administration involved the Supreme Electoral Council and other institutions.

**Limitations.** ODIHR evaluates compliance with OSCE commitments and does not determine whether all political advantages were personally directed by Erdoğan.

**Contrary evidence.** The campaign was competitive, voting was generally well organized, turnout was high, and Erdoğan required a runoff.

**Corroboration.** European Commission, BTI, Freedom House, Associated Press, and Turkish election results.

### WEB-4B-02 — Early election date and disputed presidential eligibility

**Claim and significance.** Erdoğan announced moving the election from the constitutionally scheduled 18 June date to 14 May. OSCE/ODIHR also recorded that the Supreme Electoral Council dismissed challenges arguing that he was seeking an unconstitutional third term.

These decisions affected the timing and legal basis of Erdoğan’s continuation in office.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Background and political context, pp. 6–7, lines 228–249 and 295–307.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence, with legal background inherited from earlier constitutional changes.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong: Erdoğan announced the election date and was the candidate whose eligibility was challenged.

**Limitations.** The report records the dispute and ruling but does not independently resolve the constitutional question.

**Contrary evidence.** The early election was called within the constitutional framework and reportedly had parliamentary political support; the election body formally accepted his candidacy.

**Corroboration.** Turkish Constitution; Supreme Electoral Council decisions; Venice Commission; parliamentary records.

### WEB-4B-03 — Pressure on HDP/YSP and opposition participants

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR recorded ongoing proceedings to dissolve the HDP, pressure and intimidation affecting its supporters, arrests, and the party’s decision to field candidates under the YSP banner. It also recorded prosecution of prominent opposition politicians.

This directly affected political pluralism and the ability of an important opposition constituency to compete.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Background and political context, pp. 6–7; Executive Summary, p. 3, lines 110–127 and 238–278.

**Period fit.** Direct 2023 election evidence with inherited legal proceedings.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong at national policy level; prosecution and party-closure proceedings involved courts and prosecutors. Erdoğan’s government controlled the executive environment but did not formally decide every judicial action.

**Limitations.** The report describes the proceedings and stakeholder concerns but does not adjudicate the terrorism allegations underlying the HDP case.

**Contrary evidence.** The YSP was able to contest the election and Kurdish-linked parties retained parliamentary representation.

**Corroboration.** Constitutional Court records; European Court of Human Rights; PACE; Human Rights Watch; party documents.

### WEB-4B-04 — Misuse of administrative resources and weak campaign-finance oversight

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR reported that Erdoğan frequently campaigned during official duties, including infrastructure inaugurations, and announced substantial social benefits during the campaign. It found insufficient campaign-finance regulation, weak enforcement, and limited information about campaign income and expenditure.

This blurred the distinction between state resources and party campaigning.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Executive Summary, pp. 3–4, lines 131–143.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for Erdoğan’s use of official duties and campaign appearances; broader campaign-finance regulation involved parliament and election authorities.

**Limitations.** The report identifies observed practices but does not quantify the full value of state-resource advantage.

**Contrary evidence.** Opposition parties campaigned actively and the election remained competitive.

**Corroboration.** Turkish audit and campaign-finance records; GRECO; media-monitoring reports.

### WEB-4B-05 — Public-media bias and pressure on independent outlets

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR found that public broadcasters clearly favored ruling parties and candidates, while Freedom House reported that major media were owned by businesses dependent on public tenders or close to Erdoğan. Independent outlets faced censorship, fines, prosecution, and detention of journalists.

This gave the incumbent a major informational advantage and weakened voters’ ability to compare political programs.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*; Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Turkey*.

**Locator.** OSCE/ODIHR pp. 3–4, lines 144–151; Freedom House, “Freedom of Expression and Belief,” lines 115–125.

**Period fit.** Direct election-period evidence plus broader structural context.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the presidential political environment and appointments to media regulators; ownership decisions and individual prosecutions require case-specific attribution.

**Limitations.** Freedom House is an advocacy organization and its ownership assessment should be paired with media-company, RTÜK, and public-tender records.

**Contrary evidence.** Some private media provided diverse coverage and opposition outlets continued operating.

**Corroboration.** OSCE media monitoring; Reporters Without Borders; Committee to Protect Journalists; RTÜK decisions.

### WEB-4B-06 — Disinformation Law and online censorship

**Claim and significance.** Freedom House reported that the Disinformation Law was used against opposition members and critical journalists during the election period. It criminalized deliberately false online information with prison sentences of one to three years, and hundreds of websites, articles, and social-media posts were blocked or removed.

This created a legal and practical chilling effect on online political speech.

**Source.** *Freedom on the Net 2023: Turkey*, Freedom House.

**Locator.** Country overview and key developments, lines 32–38; legal framework, lines 385–386.

**Period fit.** Law inherited from October 2022; implementation directly relevant to the 2023 election period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong at government-policy level; the law was enacted by the AK Parti-led parliamentary majority and enforced by courts, regulators, and police.

**Limitations.** Freedom House relies on civil-society, media, and legal reporting and does not provide a complete prosecution dataset.

**Contrary evidence.** Authorities argued the law was intended to combat deliberate false information and protect public order.

**Corroboration.** Turkish legal text; Constitutional Court; Article 19; OSCE/ODIHR; journalist case records.

### WEB-4B-07 — Election-administration transparency and weak remedies

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR found that election authorities managed the process efficiently but lacked transparency: the Supreme Electoral Council did not publish most decisions, public sessions were not held, complaint procedures were insufficiently reasoned, and final authority over election results was not subject to effective judicial remedy.

This weakened independent checks on electoral administration.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Executive Summary, pp. 2–4, lines 93–100 and 152–165.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The system was established by constitutional and statutory rules during Erdoğan’s period, but the Supreme Electoral Council had operational autonomy.

**Limitations.** The report assesses institutional transparency, not the validity of the final vote totals.

**Contrary evidence.** The election administration met legal deadlines, voting was generally orderly, and observers could monitor many polling stations.

**Corroboration.** Supreme Electoral Council publications; Constitutional Court; OSCE recommendations; party complaints.

### WEB-4B-08 — Unequal representation of women and minority political actors

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR reported that no woman ran for president, women represented approximately one quarter of parliamentary candidates, women held only 17 percent of the outgoing parliament, and women were severely underrepresented in ministerial and local leadership positions. It also recorded discriminatory campaign rhetoric against LGBTI people, migrants, and refugees.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Executive Summary and background, pp. 3 and 7, lines 118–130 and 289–294.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence with longer-term structural context.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Political parties and social structures contributed, while national laws, party leadership, executive appointments, and public rhetoric shaped access.

**Limitations.** Representation statistics do not alone demonstrate state discrimination; party recruitment and voter preferences also matter.

**Contrary evidence.** Women’s parliamentary representation increased from 17 to 20 percent after the election, and constitutional equality guarantees remained in force.

**Corroboration.** Parliamentary records; UN Women; party candidate lists; European Commission.

### WEB-4B-09 — Centralized executive authority and weak checks

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission found that the presidential system concentrated power in the presidency, did not ensure effective separation of powers, and left the executive primarily accountable through elections. It also reported pressure on opposition mayors, close presidential links to regulatory bodies, politicization of administration, and executive influence over judges and prosecutors.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 4–6, lines 124–140, 170–197.

**Period fit.** Structural condition operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution to Erdoğan’s presidency and governing coalition, though the system also reflected constitutional changes enacted before 2023.

**Limitations.** The Commission is an external evaluator and does not provide a complete personnel or appointment dataset.

**Contrary evidence.** Parliament, courts, municipalities, and elections retained formal powers and sometimes constrained the executive.

**Corroboration.** BTI; Venice Commission; OSCE/ODIHR; ECtHR; Turkish constitutional texts.

### WEB-4B-10 — Judicial and civic-space constraints

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR reported pressure on civil-society organizations and human-rights defenders, restrictions on expression, assembly, and association, and numerous unimplemented ECtHR judgments. The European Commission likewise found serious backsliding and executive pressure on courts.

This limited the ability of citizens and institutions to monitor and challenge government power.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*; European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*.

**Locator.** OSCE/ODIHR pp. 7–8, lines 281–287; European Commission pp. 5–7, lines 186–250.

**Period fit.** Direct 2023 evidence plus inherited structural conditions.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national institutional environment; individual legal actions require case-level attribution.

**Limitations.** International reports aggregate multiple cases and do not establish that every adverse judicial outcome was politically directed.

**Contrary evidence.** Some courts issued rights-protective decisions and civil society continued to organize.

**Corroboration.** ECtHR judgments; Constitutional Court; Amnesty; Human Rights Watch; Committee to Protect Journalists.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

The evidence environment was unusually difficult because the subject of research controlled or influenced many information channels:

- major media ownership was concentrated among government-aligned or public-contract-dependent businesses;
- journalists faced prosecution, detention, licensing pressure, and self-censorship;
- the Disinformation Law created criminal exposure for online speech;
- websites and social-media content were blocked or removed;
- public broadcasters favored the ruling coalition during the election;
- electoral bodies published limited reasoning and had weak complaint transparency;
- opposition candidates and parties faced prosecution, closure proceedings, and intimidation;
- international monitors had access, but domestic nonpartisan observation was restricted;
- official claims about security, disinformation, and public order often conflicted with NGO and opposition accounts.

The OSCE/ODIHR report is particularly valuable because it directly observed campaign, media, election-day, and dispute-resolution practices. Freedom House provides useful digital and media context but should be corroborated with Turkish legal texts, RTÜK records, court decisions, and ownership documentation.

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. OSCE/ODIHR, *Türkiye 2023 Election Observation Mission Final Report*.
2. European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*.
3. Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2023: Turkey*.
4. Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Turkey*.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- OSCE/ODIHR preliminary second-round findings.
- Constitutional Court press release on expression and assembly.
- Associated Press reports on opposition democracy proposals and the disinformation law.
- BTI 2024 Country Report.
- Committee to Protect Journalists Türkiye archive.
- Reporters Without Borders Türkiye profile.
- Amnesty International Türkiye 2023 materials.
- European Court of Human Rights records.
- Carnegie, ECFR, International Crisis Group, and academic political-history archives.

### Uninspected or still needing extraction

- Supreme Electoral Council decisions on Erdoğan’s eligibility and election complaints.
- RTÜK monitoring and sanction records for the 2023 campaign.
- Campaign-finance disclosures and public procurement records.
- Constitutional Court and ECtHR cases involving opposition politicians, journalists, and parties.
- Turkish-language media-ownership investigations.
- Parliamentary records concerning the Disinformation Law.
- Election-period arrests and prosecutions of journalists and opposition activists.
- Local-government intervention and mayoral-removal records.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources attempted: 12.
- Sources substantively opened: 12, including OSCE/ODIHR, European Commission, Freedom House, courts, NGO, academic, and media sources.
- Accepted evidence records: 10.
- Major unresolved areas: the precise effect of state resources on vote share, the legal merits of Erdoğan’s third-term eligibility, the full scale of election-period prosecutions, media-ownership and advertising data, and the practical independence of the Supreme Electoral Council and Constitutional Court.

No score is assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# Economic Well-Being and Prosperity — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Türkiye entered 2023 with high inflation, currency instability, weakened monetary-policy credibility, external-financing vulnerabilities, and an economy increasingly exposed to short-term borrowing and foreign-currency debt. The February earthquakes added a major fiscal, infrastructure, housing, and employment shock.

Erdoğan had substantial influence over economic policy. The presidential system gave him extensive appointment authority over the Central Bank and executive ministries. The European Commission reported that, before the May elections, monetary policy was subject to significant political pressure to keep real interest rates deeply negative. After the election, the government began tightening monetary policy and moving toward stabilization.

The record is mixed:

- economic activity and employment remained substantial;
- minimum wages and social transfers were increased;
- Türkiye attracted investment and maintained a large industrial and export base;
- earthquake relief received major domestic and international resources;
- but inflation remained extremely high;
- real interest rates were deeply negative before the election;
- fiscal policy became expansionary during the election and earthquake period;
- public procurement and public-private partnerships lacked transparency;
- economic institutions were not sufficiently independent;
- women, SMEs, poorer households, and earthquake-affected regions bore disproportionate burdens.

The central attribution issue is timing. Some 2023 outcomes reflected inherited policy and global conditions, while Erdoğan’s government directly shaped the pre-election monetary stance, Central Bank appointments, fiscal choices, wage policy, procurement rules, and the post-election policy reversal.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-5B-01 | High inflation and deeply negative real interest rates before the election | 5B.2, 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.9 |
| WEB-5B-02 | Post-election monetary tightening and stabilization shift | 5B.2, 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-03 | Expansionary fiscal policy, election largesse, and earthquake costs | 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.8, 5B.9 |
| WEB-5B-04 | External deficits, foreign-currency debt, and financial vulnerability | 5B.3, 5B.9, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-05 | Weak procurement transparency and political-influence risk | 5B.4, 5B.5, 5B.6 |
| WEB-5B-06 | Weak public-financial-management oversight and opaque PPPs | 5B.1, 5B.5, 5B.6, 5B.7 |
| WEB-5B-07 | Labour-market and gender gaps | 5B.8, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-08 | Earthquake reconstruction and recovery burden | 5B.6, 5B.8, 5B.9, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-09 | Economic institutional independence and appointments | 5B.2, 5B.3, 5B.7 |
| WEB-5B-10 | Continued growth and productive capacity alongside vulnerability | 5B.1, 5B.4, 5B.10 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 5B.1 — Broad and sustainable prosperity

**Disposition: mixed, with serious sustainability concerns.**

Türkiye retained a large, diversified economy, industrial capacity, employment, and investment activity. The government pursued infrastructure, domestic production, technology, and reconstruction policies.

However, the European Commission found that policy had moved away from market-oriented rules, weakened fundamentals, increased vulnerability, and relied on election-period fiscal expansion. Procurement and public-private partnership practices created risks of politically connected rents.

Evidence: WEB-5B-03, WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-06, WEB-5B-10.

### 5B.2 — Qualified economic leadership

**Disposition: adverse institutional evidence, followed by partial correction.**

Before the election, the Central Bank was under significant political pressure to maintain deeply negative real rates. Erdoğan had extensive authority to appoint and dismiss the Central Bank governor and board members. After the election, the government appointed a new economic team and began tightening policy.

The correction suggests recognition of failure, but also demonstrates that professional independence had previously been constrained.

Evidence: WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-02, WEB-5B-09.

### 5B.3 — Macroeconomic stability

**Disposition: strongly adverse before the election; partial stabilization afterward.**

The European Commission reported exceptionally low interest rates, deeply negative real rates, disrupted monetary transmission, high inflation, market fragmentation, external deficits, and growing fiscal risks. Government debt remained exposed to foreign-currency risk, and fiscal policy became expansionary in early 2023.

After the elections, monetary policy tightened and some regulatory measures were simplified. The late-year reversal did not erase the instability inherited from or aggravated by earlier policy.

Evidence: WEB-5B-01 through WEB-5B-04.

### 5B.4 — Fair economic rules

**Disposition: mixed and incomplete.**

Türkiye had a functioning private sector, industrial base, and export economy. However, the European Commission identified discriminatory domestic-price advantages, local-content requirements, regulatory exceptions, and nontransparent procurement procedures that reduced competitive neutrality.

Evidence: WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-10.

### 5B.5 — Resistance to economic capture

**Disposition: adverse evidence.**

The European Commission found that negotiated procurement procedures had increased substantially, public-private partnership contracts were not disclosed transparently, and the President could directly appoint members of the Public Procurement Board without specific educational or sector-experience requirements.

These conditions created political-influence and capture risks, although the inspected evidence does not establish that Erdoğan personally directed a specific corrupt contract in 2023.

Evidence: WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-06.

### 5B.6 — Productive public investment

**Disposition: mixed.**

Türkiye invested in infrastructure, industry, technology, and earthquake reconstruction. The economy maintained a substantial productive base.

But major public investments and PPP liabilities lacked transparency, procurement exclusions reduced competition, and the earthquake exposed serious weaknesses in building regulation and infrastructure resilience. The quality and distribution of investment require project-level examination.

Evidence: WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-06, WEB-5B-08, WEB-5B-10.

### 5B.7 — Evidence and correction

**Disposition: weak before the election; partial correction afterward.**

The Commission reported that the monetary-policy framework had lost credibility, inflation expectations had become unanchored, and the Central Bank’s policy transmission mechanism was broken. After the election, the government moved toward stabilization and policy normalization.

The evidence suggests correction, but not a fully independent or transparent evidence regime. Statistical independence, fiscal reporting, and publication of PPP liabilities remained concerns.

Evidence: WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-02, WEB-5B-06, WEB-5B-09.

### 5B.8 — Fair distribution

**Disposition: adverse and uneven.**

High inflation imposed broad purchasing-power losses. Wage increases and social benefits provided compensation but were described by the Commission as pro-cyclical in the case of minimum wages. Women faced a large labour-market participation and employment gap, while earthquake-affected regions, poorer households, refugees, SMEs, and rural communities faced unequal burdens.

Evidence: WEB-5B-03, WEB-5B-07, WEB-5B-08.

### 5B.9 — Managing economic shocks

**Disposition: mixed.**

The government responded to the earthquake through emergency spending, reconstruction commitments, and international coordination. The EU reported that a donors’ conference raised more than €6 billion.

At the same time, the earthquake increased fiscal pressure, the economy entered the disaster with significant vulnerabilities, and reconstruction procurement and implementation require further audit. Inflation and currency instability continued to affect households.

Evidence: WEB-5B-03, WEB-5B-08.

### 5B.10 — Lasting economic trajectory

**Disposition: unresolved but materially weakened at year-end.**

Türkiye retained long-term productive strengths, an industrial base, employment, and growth capacity. Yet it ended 2023 with high inflation, weakened institutional credibility, external vulnerabilities, opaque procurement risks, and large reconstruction needs.

The post-election stabilization shift improved the direction of policy, but the available evidence does not establish that the economy had already moved onto a stronger and fairer durable trajectory by the end of 2023.

Evidence: WEB-5B-01 through WEB-5B-10.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-5B-01 — Inflation and politically constrained monetary policy

**Claim and significance.** Before the May 2023 elections, Türkiye maintained exceptionally low interest rates despite very high inflation and tightening global financial conditions. The European Commission reported deeply negative real rates, disrupted monetary transmission, unanchored inflation expectations, and significant political pressure on the Central Bank.

This is the core evidence for assessing macroeconomic stability and the ruler’s influence over professional economic institutions.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** Economic criteria, pp. 60–64 and 99; especially lines 2684–2688, 2844–2852, and 4502–4524.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence with inherited policy background.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. The Commission reported presidential authority over Central Bank appointments and repeated presidential pressure for lower rates.

**Limitations.** The report is an external assessment and does not quantify the causal contribution of each presidential statement or appointment.

**Contrary evidence.** Inflation had begun to decline from its 2022 peak before rebounding, and later policy tightening represented a corrective shift.

**Corroboration.** IMF Article IV; Central Bank decisions; World Bank; TÜİK; independent inflation research.

### WEB-5B-02 — Post-election stabilization shift

**Claim and significance.** After the May elections, Türkiye began tightening monetary policy, simplifying parts of its macroprudential framework, and moving toward more stability-oriented macroeconomic policy.

This shows partial correction and a change in economic direction, although it also highlights the cost of delayed adjustment.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 9–11, 60–61, and 99; lines 359–371, 2680–2688, and 4502–4516.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the government’s post-election policy direction; Central Bank implementation was operationally delegated.

**Limitations.** The Commission’s report covers much of the year but not all late-2023 outcomes; the IMF and Central Bank should supply detailed rate and inflation analysis.

**Contrary evidence.** Tightening improved policy credibility but imposed short-term costs through credit conditions and fiscal adjustment.

**Corroboration.** IMF 2023 Article IV; Central Bank policy-rate releases; Associated Press reporting on rate increases.

### WEB-5B-03 — Expansionary fiscal policy, election spending, and earthquake pressure

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported that fiscal policy became strongly expansionary in the first half of 2023, with earthquake-related expenditure pressure added to pre-election budgetary largesse. It described minimum-wage increases as pro-cyclical and warned of rising fiscal risks.

This connects economic management to election incentives, disaster response, inflation, and distribution.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 9 and 60–65; lines 368–371, 2728–2729, and 2750–2751.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national fiscal and wage policy, though parliament and ministries also shaped appropriations and implementation.

**Limitations.** The report does not provide a complete expenditure execution analysis or separate all earthquake spending from election-related measures.

**Contrary evidence.** Fiscal expansion supported disaster relief and household incomes during a severe shock.

**Corroboration.** Turkish budget execution; IMF; World Bank earthquake-recovery documents; Ministry of Treasury and Finance records.

### WEB-5B-04 — External deficits and foreign-currency vulnerability

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye’s current-account deficit reached 5.4 percent of GDP in 2022, while the Commission noted high external deficits, reliance on short-term borrowing, a high sovereign risk premium, and substantial foreign-currency debt. Government debt had fallen as a share of GDP partly because inflation inflated the denominator, while two-thirds of the debt stock remained foreign-currency denominated.

These vulnerabilities constrained investment and increased exposure to exchange-rate and financing shocks in 2023.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 62–64, especially lines 2791–2800 and 2899–2903.

**Period fit.** Inherited macroeconomic baseline materially affecting 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong. Conditions reflected earlier policy choices and external shocks, while the 2023 government controlled monetary, fiscal, and borrowing policy.

**Limitations.** Some figures refer to 2022 or early 2023 rather than the full target year.

**Contrary evidence.** Public debt remained lower than in many comparable economies, and external deficits excluding gold and energy were smaller.

**Corroboration.** IMF; World Bank; Central Bank; Treasury debt statistics.

### WEB-5B-05 — Procurement exceptions and competition risks

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported increased use of negotiated procurement procedures, discriminatory domestic price advantages, local-content requirements, and extensive exclusions from ordinary procurement rules. It stated that these practices limited competition and transparency and strengthened allegations of political influence.

This is central to fair economic rules, capture resistance, and productive use of public resources.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Public procurement chapter, pp. 71–73, lines 3247–3258 and 3287–3339.

**Period fit.** Reporting period through mid-2023, using 2022 procurement statistics to establish the operating baseline.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution: the President could appoint the Public Procurement Board and the executive controlled the legal framework. Personal responsibility for individual tenders is not established.

**Limitations.** Much of the quantitative evidence concerns 2022, not the full 2023 calendar year.

**Contrary evidence.** Electronic procurement expanded, monitoring mechanisms existed, and contracting capacity improved in some respects.

**Corroboration.** Turkish Public Procurement Authority; Court of Accounts; procurement-monitoring reports; public tender records.

### WEB-5B-06 — Weak public-financial-management and PPP transparency

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission found weak parliamentary budget oversight, numerous procurement-law exceptions, inadequate ex-post monitoring, and a lack of transparent public-private partnership contract disclosure. It also reported that major public-investment programs lacked sufficient transparency.

This raises risks that public investment serves political networks or creates undisclosed contingent liabilities.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Public administration and public-financial-management section, p. 22, lines 960–968; procurement section, lines 3295–3300.

**Period fit.** Structural condition operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the executive and presidential system; implementation involved ministries, procurement bodies, and the Court of Accounts.

**Limitations.** The Commission reports institutional deficiencies, not a proven list of corrupt contracts or quantified fiscal losses.

**Contrary evidence.** The Turkish Court of Accounts continued ex-post controls and procurement statistics were published.

**Corroboration.** Court of Accounts reports; Treasury records; World Bank and IMF fiscal assessments.

### WEB-5B-07 — Labour-market and gender inequality

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported that the labour market strengthened but structural challenges remained; women’s participation increased while the gender gap remained above 35 percentage points, and the male–female employment and unemployment gap widened.

This shows why macroeconomic growth and employment cannot be treated as equivalent to broadly shared prosperity.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Labour-market section, pp. 67–68, lines 3068–3091; social-policy section, pp. 100–103.

**Period fit.** Direct 2023 evidence, with some indicators from the second quarter.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Outcomes reflect labour-market structures and private employers, while national education, care, wage, and employment policy shaped the environment.

**Limitations.** The report does not provide full household-income distribution or regional poverty outcomes.

**Contrary evidence.** Women’s labour-market participation increased and the government supported entrepreneurship programs.

**Corroboration.** ILO; TÜİK; World Bank; OECD; household surveys.

### WEB-5B-08 — Earthquake recovery and reconstruction burden

**Claim and significance.** The February earthquakes caused tens of thousands of deaths and widespread damage to housing, schools, health facilities, and infrastructure. The EU coordinated emergency assistance and a March 2023 donors’ conference that raised more than €6 billion.

The disaster imposed a large economic burden and tested the government’s ability to allocate resources, rebuild infrastructure, and protect affected households.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Context section, p. 3, lines 84–97.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national recovery priorities, budget allocations, and reconstruction governance; local authorities, contractors, donors, and international agencies also had important roles.

**Limitations.** The report records assistance and damage but does not assess project quality, procurement, completion, or distributional outcomes in detail.

**Contrary evidence.** International assistance began to be implemented and reconstruction became a central government priority.

**Corroboration.** World Bank earthquake-recovery project; AFAD; UNDP; EU donor records; engineering studies.

### WEB-5B-09 — Central Bank independence and appointment authority

**Claim and significance.** The Commission reported that the President had extensive authority to appoint and dismiss the Central Bank governor and board members and had repeatedly commented publicly on the need for lower interest rates. It recommended restoring functional Central Bank independence.

This directly connects institutional personnel decisions to monetary credibility and macroeconomic performance.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Economic and monetary policy chapter, pp. 99–100, lines 4510–4524.

**Period fit.** Structural condition directly relevant to 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong.

**Limitations.** The source does not provide a complete appointment chronology or performance assessment for every governor.

**Contrary evidence.** After the election, new economic personnel and tighter policy indicated some delegation to more orthodox professionals.

**Corroboration.** Presidential appointment decrees; Central Bank biographies; IMF; Treasury records.

### WEB-5B-10 — Productive capacity and continued economic activity

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission described Türkiye’s economy as well advanced and the labour market as strengthened, while the EBRD and World Bank continued to treat Türkiye as a major emerging-market economy with substantial production, investment, and growth capacity.

This is important counterevidence against describing 2023 as an economy without productive achievements.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission; EBRD Türkiye macroeconomic research.

**Locator.** European Commission economic criteria, pp. 60–70 and 67–68; EBRD Türkiye forecast materials.

**Period fit.** 2023 and inherited structural capacity.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Productive capacity reflected long-term private-sector, industrial, demographic, and global conditions, not only Erdoğan’s decisions.

**Limitations.** Aggregate growth and production do not establish household prosperity, real-wage gains, or fair distribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Inflation, currency instability, external deficits, and gender inequality weakened the quality and distribution of growth.

**Corroboration.** World Bank WDI; TÜİK; EBRD; OECD; ILO.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Economic evidence was difficult to interpret because:

- official inflation and national statistics were politically contested;
- nominal-dollar changes mixed inflation, exchange rates, and real activity;
- economic policy changed sharply after the May elections;
- many indicators were reported with delays or revised later;
- public procurement and PPP contracts were not fully transparent;
- the earthquake disrupted economic activity and data collection;
- household purchasing power and regional effects were less visible than GDP and headline growth;
- official claims about success required comparison with IMF, World Bank, EBRD, ILO, independent inflation estimates, and household data.

The European Commission report is strong for institutional and policy assessment, but full 2023 macroeconomic evaluation requires the IMF Article IV, Central Bank decisions, Treasury execution data, TÜİK series, and household-distribution evidence.

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*.
2. World Bank, *Macro Poverty Outlook for Türkiye: April 2023* — opened as supporting macroeconomic context.
3. World Bank, *Türkiye Earthquake Recovery and Reconstruction Project* — opened as recovery context.
4. EBRD, Türkiye macroeconomic forecast materials.
5. TÜİK official statistics portal.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- World Bank Turkey Country Economic Memorandum.
- Bertelsmann Stiftung BTI 2022 and BTI 2024 country reports.
- Al Jazeera Centre for Studies political-economy paper.
- Academic political-economy theses and monographs.
- Carnegie, Brookings, Chatham House, and EBRD research collections.

### Uninspected or still needing extraction

- IMF 2023 Article IV consultation.
- Central Bank policy-rate decisions and appointment records.
- Turkish Treasury budget execution and debt reports.
- 2023 Public Procurement Authority monitoring report.
- Court of Accounts audits.
- World Bank earthquake recovery implementation documents.
- ILO wage and employment analysis.
- Ministry of Treasury and Finance Medium-Term Program 2023–2025.
- Transparency International CPI 2023 report.
- Household income, real-wage, poverty, and regional-distribution data.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources attempted: 12.
- Sources substantively opened: 8.
- Accepted evidence records: 10.
- Principal remaining gaps: full-year 2023 inflation and real-wage outcomes, IMF assessment of policy credibility, Central Bank appointment chronology, budget execution, PPP liabilities, earthquake-reconstruction spending, household poverty, and whether post-election tightening had begun to improve real incomes by year-end.

No score is assigned.
