--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# Social Well-Being and Human Development — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Türkiye entered 2023 with substantial national service capacity, a large social-assistance system, broad health and education infrastructure, and experience hosting millions of refugees. Erdoğan’s presidential system gave the executive strong control over ministries, social policy, emergency administration, appointments, and reconstruction.

The defining social-welfare event was the February earthquake disaster. The earthquakes killed tens of thousands of people and destroyed homes, schools, health facilities, water systems, and local livelihoods. They exposed both state capacity and severe inequalities in access to shelter, health care, education, protection, and administrative assistance.

The evidence is mixed:

- Türkiye maintained significant health, education, social-protection, and refugee-support systems;
- the government coordinated major domestic and international relief efforts;
- international donors pledged more than €6 billion for recovery;
- Türkiye continued hosting approximately 3.6 million refugees;
- but earthquake-affected people faced shelter, sanitation, health, education, disability, and livelihood gaps;
- refugees, Roma, women, children, LGBTIQ people, and persons with disabilities experienced disproportionate hardship;
- service quality and access varied sharply by region;
- independent evaluation and transparent reconstruction monitoring remain incomplete.

The period should not be assessed through national averages alone. The key questions are whether services reached affected and disadvantaged people, whether access was affordable and dignified, and whether emergency and social institutions were durable beyond Erdoğan’s personal control.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-6B-01 | Earthquake destruction and national recovery burden | 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.6, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-02 | International relief and reconstruction coordination | 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.6, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-03 | Refugee hosting and incomplete integration | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.7, 6B.8 |
| WEB-6B-04 | Disproportionate effects on Roma and vulnerable groups | 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.8 |
| WEB-6B-05 | Damaged health infrastructure and reduced access | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6 |
| WEB-6B-06 | Gender and reproductive-health gaps after the earthquake | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.8 |
| WEB-6B-07 | Disability-access failures in the earthquake response | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.8 |
| WEB-6B-08 | Education disruption and regional inequality | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-09 | Weak administrative accountability and evaluation | 6B.4, 6B.5, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-10 | Social-protection and service outcomes requiring official-data extraction | 6B.2, 6B.5, 6B.10 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 6B.1 — Welfare as a governing purpose

**Disposition: substantial formal and operational evidence, but mixed execution.**

The government treated earthquake relief, reconstruction, refugee support, health, education, and social assistance as major policy priorities. Türkiye also coordinated extensive international assistance.

However, the disaster exposed preventable service failures and unequal access. The evidence supports a genuine welfare role for the state but not consistently effective or equitable delivery.

Evidence: WEB-6B-01, WEB-6B-02, WEB-6B-03, WEB-6B-09.

### 6B.2 — Access to essential services

**Disposition: mixed, with severe disaster-related gaps.**

Türkiye maintained broad national health, education, and social-protection systems. Yet the earthquakes damaged health facilities and schools, displaced millions, disrupted water and sanitation, and reduced access to sexual and reproductive health services.

Refugees, disabled people, rural residents, women, and children faced additional barriers.

Evidence: WEB-6B-03, WEB-6B-05 through WEB-6B-08.

### 6B.3 — Priority for vulnerable groups

**Disposition: materially inadequate in the earthquake response.**

The European Commission reported disproportionate effects on Roma communities. Other sources identified heightened hardship for refugees, people with disabilities, women, children, and rural residents.

Türkiye and international partners provided targeted assistance, but the evidence shows unequal access and insufficient inclusion of vulnerable groups in relief and service design.

Evidence: WEB-6B-03, WEB-6B-04, WEB-6B-06, WEB-6B-07.

### 6B.4 — Professional service delivery

**Disposition: mixed and insufficiently documented.**

Türkiye had substantial administrative and professional service capacity, but the European Commission reported politicized public administration, insufficient accountability, weak public-financial-management reform, and limited transparency in major public investment.

The evidence does not yet permit a complete assessment of staffing, qualifications, procurement, or performance across health, education, and social ministries.

Evidence: WEB-6B-09; personnel and execution records remain needed.

### 6B.5 — Measurement and correction

**Disposition: unresolved, with accountability concerns.**

Official statistical systems and service reporting existed, and TÜİK published 2023 social-protection, income, poverty, and life-satisfaction releases. However, the inspected evidence does not yet show whether the government transparently evaluated earthquake programs, corrected failures, or published complete regional and group-level outcomes.

Evidence: WEB-6B-09, WEB-6B-10.

### 6B.6 — Protection during crises

**Disposition: mixed but central failures remain.**

The government and international organizations mobilized substantial emergency relief after the earthquakes. But damaged health infrastructure, shelter and sanitation shortages, disability-access failures, refugee displacement, and weakened reproductive-health access indicate that crisis protection was uneven and sometimes inadequate.

Evidence: WEB-6B-01, WEB-6B-02, WEB-6B-05 through WEB-6B-08.

### 6B.7 — No political allocation of welfare

**Disposition: unresolved and requiring targeted investigation.**

Türkiye operated formal social-protection and emergency-assistance systems. The available sources do not establish that welfare was systematically allocated according to political loyalty or withheld as punishment in 2023.

However, centralized administration, pressure on opposition municipalities, weak accountability, and nontransparent reconstruction procurement create risks that require examination of beneficiary lists, permit rules, housing allocations, and appeals.

Evidence gap: WEB-6B-09 and WEB-6B-10.

### 6B.8 — Dignity and equal opportunity

**Disposition: adverse evidence for vulnerable groups.**

Refugees, Roma, women, persons with disabilities, and LGBTIQ people faced discrimination, inadequate access, or disproportionate hardship. Formal equality and assistance programs existed, but practical dignity and equal access were not consistently achieved.

Evidence: WEB-6B-03, WEB-6B-04, WEB-6B-06, WEB-6B-07.

### 6B.9 — Durable social institutions

**Disposition: mixed and unresolved.**

Türkiye possessed durable institutions such as AFAD, ministries, hospitals, schools, social-assistance agencies, and refugee-support programs. International support also created institutional capacity.

But centralized policymaking, politicization, weak audit, procurement exceptions, and incomplete reconstruction monitoring raise questions about whether these institutions were sufficiently independent, professional, and resilient beyond personal rule.

Evidence: WEB-6B-02, WEB-6B-09, WEB-6B-10.

### 6B.10 — Lasting life chances

**Disposition: unresolved, with severe regional and group disparities.**

National service capacity and refugee assistance provided important foundations for life chances. Yet the earthquake caused massive losses in housing, health, education, livelihoods, and physical security. By the end of 2023, the evidence did not establish that affected households, refugees, disabled people, women, Roma, and rural communities had durably recovered.

Evidence: WEB-6B-01, WEB-6B-03, WEB-6B-05 through WEB-6B-10.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-6B-01 — Earthquake destruction and social-welfare shock

**Claim and significance.** The February 2023 earthquakes killed tens of thousands of people and destroyed or damaged housing, schools, health facilities, and other infrastructure. The disaster displaced millions and made relief, reconstruction, health, education, housing, and social protection central government responsibilities.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** Context section, p. 3, lines 84–97.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national response priorities and executive administration; responsibility for individual building failures and local service breakdowns was distributed.

**Limitations.** The report records broad damage and assistance but not complete household-level outcomes.

**Contrary evidence.** Large-scale national and international relief operations began, and reconstruction became a stated government priority.

**Corroboration.** World Bank earthquake-recovery project; UN Türkiye response review; AFAD; UNFPA; engineering assessments.

### WEB-6B-02 — International recovery and humanitarian coordination

**Claim and significance.** The EU and Türkiye coordinated an international donors’ conference in March 2023 that raised more than €6 billion for recovery and reconstruction. The EU had already mobilized substantial assistance through humanitarian funding and the Civil Protection Mechanism.

This demonstrates significant external support and state capacity to coordinate recovery, while also creating a need to track implementation, distribution, and accountability.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** p. 3, lines 91–97.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national coordination and government priorities; donors and international agencies controlled much of the financing and implementation.

**Limitations.** Pledges are not equivalent to completed projects or improved household welfare.

**Contrary evidence.** The Commission stated that implementation of pledged European assistance had begun.

**Corroboration.** World Bank project documents; UN response review; EU donor-conference records.

### WEB-6B-03 — Refugee hosting and incomplete integration

**Claim and significance.** Türkiye continued hosting approximately 3.6 million refugees from Syria and other countries. The EU reported that almost €10 billion had been mobilized for refugees and host communities since 2012, with approximately €7 billion disbursed by September 2023. The Commission nevertheless found that integration measures and access to public health remained inadequate.

This is important positive and adverse evidence for social protection, service access, and equal dignity.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 4 and 7, lines 117–122 and 282–288.

**Period fit.** Direct 2023 evidence with long-term program context.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for national refugee policy and coordination; EU funding and international agencies played major roles.

**Limitations.** Aggregate refugee numbers and funding do not measure actual service uptake, living conditions, or regional distribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye sustained one of the world’s largest refugee-hosting efforts and received substantial international support.

**Corroboration.** UNHCR Annual Results Report 2023; UNICEF; EU Facility for Refugees in Türkiye; Turkish migration authorities.

### WEB-6B-04 — Disproportionate effects on Roma and marginalized groups

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported that Roma communities were already excluded from formal employment and experienced severe deterioration in living conditions. The February earthquakes affected Roma people disproportionately, particularly in livelihoods, housing, basic needs, health, women’s conditions, and children’s welfare.

This shows that national emergency programs did not affect all groups equally.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 6–7, lines 251–262.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence with inherited disadvantage.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. National social policy and emergency administration shaped outcomes, while local conditions and pre-existing discrimination also mattered.

**Limitations.** The report gives qualitative findings rather than a full Roma-specific expenditure or outcome dataset.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye adopted a Roma strategy for 2023–2030 and an action plan for 2023–2025.

**Corroboration.** Ministry of Family and Social Services reports; Roma civil-society organizations; UN agencies.

### WEB-6B-05 — Damaged health infrastructure and reduced access

**Claim and significance.** The earthquakes severely damaged health infrastructure in affected provinces and caused the loss or displacement of health staff. Some facilities stopped operating or operated at reduced capacity, reducing access to health and sexual and reproductive-health services.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Turkey*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** Health and reproductive-rights section, lines 338–340.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for emergency health coordination, resource allocation, and reconstruction; the disaster itself was an external shock.

**Limitations.** The report does not provide complete facility-by-facility service data or distinguish temporary from permanent closures.

**Contrary evidence.** Emergency health services continued in many areas, and international assistance supported recovery.

**Corroboration.** WHO; UNFPA; World Bank; Turkish Ministry of Health; AFAD.

### WEB-6B-06 — Gender and reproductive-health disparities

**Claim and significance.** The U.S. State Department reported that access to sexual and reproductive-health services deteriorated after the earthquakes, especially for refugee women and girls and rural residents. The European Commission also identified serious gender-based discrimination and insufficient protection for vulnerable groups.

This is important evidence that formal service availability did not guarantee equal access.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Turkey*, U.S. Department of State, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** Lines 326–340.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. National health and social-service systems were responsible for crisis provision, while local facilities and international organizations also affected delivery.

**Limitations.** Some gender and service-access findings rely on NGO reporting and do not establish nationwide incidence.

**Contrary evidence.** Emergency contraception and post-exposure prophylaxis were reportedly available for survivors of sexual violence.

**Corroboration.** UNFPA Türkiye; WHO; Turkish Women’s Associations Federation; Ministry of Health.

### WEB-6B-07 — Disability-access failures

**Claim and significance.** Amnesty International reported that earthquake relief distribution did not adequately account for the rights and specific needs of persons with disabilities. Injured people faced difficulties obtaining prostheses and assistive devices, and government figures indicated that many of the injured were likely to live with disabilities.

This indicates a failure of inclusive crisis design and threatens long-term life chances.

**Source.** *The State of the World’s Human Rights; Türkiye 2023*, Amnesty International, reproduced by ecoi.net.

**Locator.** Disability and earthquake-response section, lines 47–48.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence, later retrospective.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. National emergency standards and social services mattered, while local relief providers and international agencies shared implementation.

**Limitations.** NGO reporting does not provide a complete national audit of disability-service delivery.

**Contrary evidence.** Government and international actors provided medical and humanitarian aid, including some assistive services.

**Corroboration.** UN agencies; Ministry of Family and Social Services; disability organizations; World Bank recovery documents.

### WEB-6B-08 — Education disruption and regional inequality

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported that access to education in southeastern Türkiye was significantly affected by the earthquakes. Damage to schools, displacement, teacher loss, and disrupted housing affected children’s continuity of education and regional equality.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Education section, p. 95, lines 4325–4326; earthquake context, p. 3.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for national education policy, school reconstruction, teacher deployment, and emergency planning; local authorities and donors also mattered.

**Limitations.** The accessible report does not quantify missed school days, enrollment changes, or learning loss.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye and international partners continued education and reconstruction programs in affected areas.

**Corroboration.** UNICEF Türkiye; UNESCO; Ministry of National Education; World Bank recovery project.

### WEB-6B-09 — Administrative accountability and professional delivery concerns

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission found highly centralized policymaking, insufficient administrative accountability, politicization of public administration, weak public-financial-management reform, and inadequate transparency in major public-investment programs.

These conditions affect whether social services and reconstruction are delivered professionally rather than through patronage or opaque administrative decisions.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** Public administration and financial-management sections, pp. 21–22, lines 177–185 and 960–968.

**Period fit.** Structural condition operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution to the presidential executive; individual service failures require agency-level evidence.

**Limitations.** This is an institutional assessment rather than a direct evaluation of every social-service program.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye maintained a large administrative system and the Court of Accounts continued ex-post controls.

**Corroboration.** AFAD reports; Ministry of Family and Social Services reports; Court of Accounts; World Bank implementation reviews.

### WEB-6B-10 — Official social-outcome measurement

**Claim and significance.** TÜİK maintained official 2023 releases for life satisfaction, social protection, income distribution, and poverty and living conditions. These provide a basis for measuring welfare outcomes, but the accessible pages did not expose the underlying tables in this pass.

This is a source and measurement record rather than a substantive outcome conclusion.

**Source.** TÜİK, 2023 Life Satisfaction, Social Protection, Income Distribution, and Poverty and Living Conditions releases.

**Locator.** Official release pages:
- [Life Satisfaction Survey 2023](https://veriportali.tuik.gov.tr/en/press/49692)
- [Social Protection Statistics 2023](https://veriportali.tuik.gov.tr/en/press/53560)
- [Income Distribution Statistics 2023](https://veriportali.tuik.gov.tr/en/press/53840)
- [Poverty and Living Conditions Statistics 2023](https://veriportali.tuik.gov.tr/en/press/53841)

**Period fit.** Direct target-year official measurement.

**Ruler attribution.** State-level statistical production; attribution to Erdoğan concerns the statistical and social-policy institutions, not the measured outcomes themselves.

**Limitations.** Tables, definitions, survey timing, earthquake exclusions, and regional breakdowns require direct extraction.

**Contrary evidence.** International datasets may provide alternative estimates and methodological checks.

**Corroboration.** World Bank, UNDP, Eurostat, UNICEF, UNHCR, and household surveys.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Social-wellbeing evidence was difficult to interpret because:

- the earthquake disrupted facilities, populations, and administrative data;
- national averages concealed sharp regional and group-level differences;
- official announcements often described inputs or pledges rather than completed delivery;
- refugee, disability, Roma, gender, and rural outcomes were incompletely measured;
- donor-funded programs made attribution difficult;
- municipal, national, NGO, and international agencies shared responsibility;
- independent reporting and civil-society monitoring operated under political pressure;
- TÜİK release pages did not expose all underlying tables during inspection.

The strongest evidence concerns earthquake damage, refugee support, health-system disruption, disability exclusion, education disruption, and institutional capacity. Full assessment of poverty, income distribution, life satisfaction, social-protection coverage, and service uptake requires extraction of official 2023 datasets and comparison with UN, World Bank, and household-survey measures.

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*.
2. U.S. Department of State, *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Turkey*.
3. Amnesty International, *The State of the World’s Human Rights; Türkiye 2023*.
4. United Nations in Türkiye, earthquake-response materials.
5. UNFPA Türkiye earthquake-response materials.
6. World Bank Türkiye earthquake-recovery project record.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- UNICEF Türkiye country data and reports.
- UNHCR Türkiye annual results materials.
- TÜİK 2023 social-outcome release pages.
- World Bank country and recovery materials.
- Amnesty, Human Rights Watch, CPJ, Freedom House, and RSF country archives.
- Academic political-economy and governance studies.

### Uninspected or still needing extraction

- AFAD 2023 activity report.
- Ministry of Family and Social Services annual and financial reports.
- UNHCR 2023 annual results report.
- TÜİK underlying tables for poverty, income distribution, social protection, and life satisfaction.
- Ministry of National Education education outcomes.
- Ministry of Health facility and staffing data.
- World Bank earthquake project appraisal and implementation documents.
- Household survey evidence for affected provinces and vulnerable groups.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources attempted: 12.
- Sources substantively opened: 8.
- Accepted evidence records: 10.
- Principal remaining gaps: completed reconstruction and housing delivery, health and education restoration, social-protection coverage, poverty and income changes, refugee outcomes, disability services, child welfare, and whether aid allocation was politically neutral across national and opposition-governed municipalities.

No score is assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty — Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Türkiye, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Erdoğan was president throughout 2023 and exercised extensive authority over appointments, ministries, regulators, public procurement, monetary institutions, state media, security agencies, and the executive administration.

The evidence supports a distinction between:

- documented institutional weaknesses that created opportunities for corruption, favoritism, and concealment;
- political conduct that benefited Erdoğan’s incumbency;
- allegations involving Erdoğan, his family, or associates; and
- proven personal enrichment or criminal wrongdoing.

The inspected material strongly documents weak anti-corruption institutions, opaque procurement, insufficient campaign-finance transparency, executive pressure on courts, and a lack of independent accountability. It does not, by itself, establish that Erdoğan personally accepted a bribe, unlawfully acquired assets, or directed a specific corrupt contract in 2023.

The most defensible factual conclusion is therefore institutional and attribution-calibrated: Erdoğan presided over and exercised substantial influence within a system that offered weak independent scrutiny and high opportunities for clientelism, while direct personal enrichment requires additional primary financial, judicial, or investigative evidence.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-7B-01 | Türkiye remained at an early stage in anti-corruption policy | 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.10 |
| WEB-7B-02 | Corruption investigations and prosecutions faced undue influence risks | 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-03 | Procurement rules and negotiated procedures created favoritism risks | 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-04 | Presidential appointment authority weakened institutional independence | 7B.5, 7B.6, 7B.7 |
| WEB-7B-05 | Campaign-finance disclosure and oversight were inadequate | 7B.1, 7B.3, 7B.7, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-06 | Erdoğan used official functions and administrative resources during the election | 7B.1, 7B.8, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-07 | Monetary policy reflected presidential influence and later correction | 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.8 |
| WEB-7B-08 | Courts and rights institutions issued findings that the executive did not fully implement | 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7 |
| WEB-7B-09 | Evidence of direct personal enrichment remained unestablished in inspected sources | 7B.4, 7B.5, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-10 | Country-level corruption indicators provide context but not personal attribution | 7B.10 |

## 3. Disposition by lens

### 7B.1 — Truthfulness

**Disposition: mixed and insufficiently resolved.**

The inspected evidence documents disputed campaign claims, weak campaign-finance disclosure, and state-resource advantages. OSCE/ODIHR recorded that Erdoğan’s opponent sued over a doctored video falsely depicting him campaigning with terrorists.

These findings establish misleading political communication in the campaign environment, but do not establish that Erdoğan personally created or knowingly repeated every false claim. Direct verification of Erdoğan’s major 2023 statements remains necessary.

Evidence: WEB-7B-05, WEB-7B-06, WEB-7B-09.

### 7B.2 — Correcting falsehoods and errors

**Disposition: adverse institutional evidence.**

The European Commission found that Türkiye had made no meaningful progress in anti-corruption policy and that the government had not fully addressed structural judicial and accountability problems. It also reported nonimplementation of certain ECtHR rulings.

The record does not show a broad pattern of Erdoğan personally acknowledging and correcting falsehoods or errors. The post-election economic-policy reversal provides one example of practical correction, but not necessarily personal admission of error.

Evidence: WEB-7B-01, WEB-7B-07, WEB-7B-08.

### 7B.3 — Conflicts of interest

**Disposition: high institutional risk; personal conflict evidence incomplete.**

The European Commission reported that procurement exemptions, domestic-price advantages, opaque PPPs, and presidential appointment powers weakened safeguards against conflicts of interest. The inspected material does not establish a specific 2023 conflict involving Erdoğan or his family.

Asset declarations, beneficial-ownership records, family business interests, and procurement-beneficiary data remain necessary.

Evidence: WEB-7B-03, WEB-7B-04, WEB-7B-09.

### 7B.4 — Personal profit from office

**Disposition: no accepted direct finding in this pass.**

No inspected primary record, court judgment, audit, or independently corroborated investigation established that Erdoğan or a close family member personally received unlawful profit from office during 2023.

This is a genuine evidence gap, not exculpatory proof. The institutional environment created opportunities for personal or network enrichment, but opportunities must not be converted into a personal-corruption finding.

Evidence: WEB-7B-03, WEB-7B-09, WEB-7B-10.

### 7B.5 — Nepotism and loyalist appointments

**Disposition: institutional concern; personal nepotism not established.**

The European Commission reported politicization of public administration, insufficient merit-based criteria in judicial appointments, and extensive presidential appointment authority. These findings support concern about loyalty-based governance.

The inspected material does not establish a specific 2023 appointment of an unqualified relative or business associate for personal gain.

Evidence: WEB-7B-04, WEB-7B-09.

### 7B.6 — Independent investigation

**Disposition: weak and adverse.**

The Commission found undue executive influence over investigations and prosecutions, weak judicial independence, and incomplete civilian oversight. These conditions impaired the ability of courts, prosecutors, auditors, and investigators to examine Erdoğan, associates, or politically connected actors independently.

Evidence: WEB-7B-01, WEB-7B-02, WEB-7B-04, WEB-7B-08.

### 7B.7 — Obstruction and concealment

**Disposition: adverse institutional evidence.**

The Commission reported that Türkiye had not implemented certain ECtHR rulings, that corruption investigations could face undue influence, and that public institutions lacked transparency. OSCE/ODIHR found delayed publication of campaign-finance audits and weak election-dispute remedies.

The evidence supports barriers to accountability but does not establish that Erdoğan personally issued a concealment order or obstructed a specific investigation in 2023.

Evidence: WEB-7B-01, WEB-7B-02, WEB-7B-05, WEB-7B-08.

### 7B.8 — Promises and good faith

**Disposition: mixed.**

Erdoğan’s government changed course after the May election toward tighter monetary policy and greater stabilization, after maintaining deeply negative real rates and publicly favoring low interest rates. This may represent pragmatic correction, but it also raises questions about whether pre-election policy was presented candidly and whether the costs were concealed from the public.

Evidence: WEB-7B-06, WEB-7B-07.

### 7B.9 — Favoritism and clientelism

**Disposition: strong institutional risk; direct personal benefit unproven.**

The European Commission reported increasing use of negotiated procurement, nontransparent PPPs, domestic-price advantages, and presidential control over procurement-board appointments. OSCE/ODIHR found that Erdoğan used official functions and state-linked resources during the campaign.

These facts establish a political environment conducive to favoritism and clientelism. They do not prove that Erdoğan personally directed or profited from a specific contract or licensing decision.

Evidence: WEB-7B-03, WEB-7B-04, WEB-7B-05, WEB-7B-06.

### 7B.10 — Ethical example and public trust

**Disposition: adverse institutional environment, personal conclusion unresolved.**

Erdoğan’s government presided over weak anti-corruption safeguards, limited transparency, politicized administration, pressure on courts, and unequal scrutiny. These conditions likely weakened public trust and normalized impunity risks.

However, country-level CPI, WGI, and V-Dem indicators cannot establish personal dishonesty. The personal-integrity conclusion requires direct evidence concerning Erdoğan’s statements, assets, family interests, appointments, and responses to specific findings.

Evidence: WEB-7B-01 through WEB-7B-10.

## 4. Developed evidence records

### WEB-7B-01 — Weak national anti-corruption policy

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission assessed Türkiye as remaining at an early stage in combating corruption and reported no progress during the relevant period. It identified missing prevention policy, institutional gaps, legislative deficiencies, insufficient transparency, and weak accountability.

This establishes the integrity environment in which Erdoğan and close associates operated.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission, 8 November 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 5–6, lines 198–209.

**Period fit.** Reporting period through June 2023, with structural relevance throughout 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution to the executive and governing coalition; not proof of Erdoğan’s personal corruption.

**Limitations.** Country-level institutional findings cannot establish individual misconduct.

**Contrary evidence.** Türkiye remained party to international anti-corruption conventions and conducted some organized-crime operations.

**Corroboration.** GRECO; Transparency International CPI 2023; World Bank governance indicators; Turkish Court of Accounts.

### WEB-7B-02 — Undue influence over corruption investigations and prosecutions

**Claim and significance.** The Commission reported that legal and institutional limitations allowed undue influence during the investigation and prosecution phases of corruption cases. It also found that judicial independence was undermined by executive pressure on judges and prosecutors.

This directly affects whether Erdoğan, family members, associates, or political allies could be investigated independently.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 5–6, lines 186–208.

**Period fit.** Structural condition operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the institutional environment; no individual obstruction order is established.

**Limitations.** The report does not identify every affected case or prove that all corruption investigations were interfered with.

**Contrary evidence.** Courts and prosecutors continued to investigate some cases, and formal legal remedies remained available.

**Corroboration.** GRECO compliance reports; ECtHR; Constitutional Court; Turkish judicial records.

### WEB-7B-03 — Procurement and favoritism risks

**Claim and significance.** The Commission reported extensive procurement exclusions, increased use of negotiated procedures, domestic-price advantages, local-content requirements, opaque PPP contracts, and allegations of political influence over tenders.

These facts create risks of favoritism, clientelism, conflicts of interest, and improper public benefit.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 71–73, lines 3247–3258 and 3287–3339.

**Period fit.** Reporting period through mid-2023, with quantitative baseline data primarily from 2022.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution because presidential authorities shaped appointments and procurement rules. No specific Erdoğan-directed contract was identified.

**Limitations.** Institutional risk is not proof of a particular corrupt award or personal enrichment.

**Contrary evidence.** Electronic procurement expanded, monitoring mechanisms existed, and a legal complaints process operated.

**Corroboration.** Turkish Public Procurement Authority; Court of Accounts; procurement records; investigative reporting.

### WEB-7B-04 — Presidential control over appointments and regulators

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported that most regulatory authorities remained linked to the presidency, public administration was politicized, and merit-based criteria for judicial recruitment and promotion remained inadequate. The report also noted presidential authority to appoint key institutional officials.

This is relevant to loyalist appointments, conflicts, and the capacity of institutions to investigate the ruler.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 4–6, lines 132–140, 177–197, and 3315–3318.

**Period fit.** Structural condition operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong.

**Limitations.** The source does not provide a complete 2023 personnel database or prove that every appointment was politically motivated.

**Contrary evidence.** Some agencies retained technical staff and formal statutory mandates.

**Corroboration.** Presidential decrees; agency appointment records; Constitutional Court; GRECO; parliamentary records.

### WEB-7B-05 — Weak campaign-finance transparency

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR found that campaign-finance rules lacked comprehensive regulation, spending limits, timely reporting, and effective independent oversight. Voters had limited information about campaign income and expenditure, audit publication was delayed, and presidential campaign violations lacked clear sanctions.

This is relevant to truthfulness, political financing, concealed benefits, and accountability.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Election Observation Mission Final Report*, OSCE/ODIHR, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** pp. 18–20, lines 846–906.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the incumbent’s campaign environment; the legal framework involved parliament, the Supreme Electoral Council, and the Constitutional Court.

**Limitations.** The report does not prove that Erdoğan personally violated campaign-finance rules.

**Contrary evidence.** Formal donation limits and dedicated bank-account rules existed.

**Corroboration.** Supreme Electoral Council audit results; Constitutional Court party-finance records; GRECO.

### WEB-7B-06 — Use of official functions and state resources during the campaign

**Claim and significance.** OSCE/ODIHR observed Erdoğan campaigning during official duties, including infrastructure inaugurations and announcements of social benefits. It concluded that this blurred the line between party and state and provided the incumbent with an undue advantage.

This is a directly documented example of public office being used in a way that benefited political power.

**Source.** OSCE/ODIHR, *Final Report*, 29 September 2023.

**Locator.** Executive Summary, pp. 3–4, lines 131–143.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong: the conduct concerned Erdoğan personally using presidential functions while campaigning.

**Limitations.** The report does not quantify the monetary value or establish a criminal violation.

**Contrary evidence.** The election remained competitive and the incumbent needed a runoff.

**Corroboration.** Presidential schedule; public-infrastructure announcements; campaign records; Turkish media monitoring.

### WEB-7B-07 — Monetary-policy reversal and good-faith questions

**Claim and significance.** The European Commission reported that monetary policy before the election maintained deeply negative real rates under political pressure, while policy tightened after the election. The change suggests a correction but also raises questions about whether prior policy was maintained for political advantage and whether its costs were candidly explained.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 9–11, 60–64, and 99–100, especially lines 359–371, 2684–2688, and 4502–4524.

**Period fit.** Direct target-year evidence with inherited policy background.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for presidential influence over appointments and policy direction; Central Bank officials implemented the policy.

**Limitations.** The record does not prove that Erdoğan’s motive was electoral advantage or that he knowingly misrepresented economic facts.

**Contrary evidence.** The post-election shift may reflect good-faith stabilization in response to changed circumstances.

**Corroboration.** IMF Article IV; Central Bank rate decisions; Treasury statements; independent economic analysis.

### WEB-7B-08 — Nonimplementation of rights judgments and accountability findings

**Claim and significance.** The Commission reported that Türkiye had not implemented certain ECtHR rulings, including a July 2022 Grand Chamber ruling, and that judicial and fundamental-rights conditions had not improved.

Although these are not personal-corruption findings, nonimplementation illustrates the broader accountability environment in which the ruler and close associates operated.

**Source.** *Türkiye 2023 Report*, European Commission.

**Locator.** pp. 5–7, lines 186–197 and 223–236.

**Period fit.** Structural condition relevant to 2023; some judgments predated the target year.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution; personal responsibility depends on the executive’s role in implementation decisions.

**Limitations.** The source does not establish Erdoğan’s personal involvement in each nonimplementation decision.

**Contrary evidence.** Some domestic courts continued to issue rights-related decisions and formal judicial institutions remained in place.

**Corroboration.** ECtHR Committee of Ministers; Constitutional Court annual materials; Council of Europe.

### WEB-7B-09 — Direct personal enrichment not established in inspected evidence

**Claim.** The inspected sources established procurement opacity, campaign-finance weaknesses, executive influence, and institutional corruption risks, but did not establish through a primary record, final court judgment, audit, or independently corroborated investigation that Erdoğan or a close family member unlawfully profited from office in 2023.

**Why it matters.** This prevents country-level corruption indicators and institutional risks from being misrepresented as proof of personal wrongdoing.

**Source base.** European Commission, OSCE/ODIHR, GRECO, Transparency International, World Bank governance materials, and rights/institutional reports inspected in this pass.

**Period fit.** 2023 and ruler-period context.

**Ruler attribution.** The absence of an accepted finding is not exculpatory; it reflects the evidence inspected and the need for stronger personal financial records.

**Limitations.** Asset declarations, beneficial ownership, family-company records, procurement beneficiaries, tax records, and investigative case files were not fully inspected.

**Contrary evidence.** Institutional evidence creates plausible risk pathways for favoritism and enrichment.

**Corroboration needed.** Turkish asset declarations; company registries; procurement and PPP records; court filings; investigative investigations with underlying documents.

### WEB-7B-10 — Country-level integrity indicators as context only

**Claim.** CPI, WGI, V-Dem, and similar measures describe national corruption, accountability, and governance conditions, but do not by themselves establish Erdoğan’s personal honesty, enrichment, or intent.

**Why it matters.** These indicators can triangulate institutional context but must not be treated as independent evidence of personal misconduct.

**Source.** Transparency International CPI 2023; World Bank Worldwide Governance Indicators; V-Dem country data.

**Period fit.** Country-level 2023 context.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak for personal attribution; stronger for the policy and institutional environment over which the executive had substantial influence.

**Limitations.** Perception-based and aggregate indicators do not identify specific conduct or actors.

**Contrary evidence.** They can miss enforcement improvements, isolated integrity actions, or unreported personal misconduct.

**Corroboration.** Primary audits, judgments, asset records, and procurement documents are required.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Personal-integrity research was unusually difficult because:

- access to asset declarations and beneficial-ownership information was limited;
- campaign-finance reports and audits were delayed or incomplete;
- public procurement and PPP contracts were not fully transparent;
- judicial and prosecutorial independence was contested;
- journalists and civil-society investigators faced legal and political pressure;
- allegations involving family members or associates were politically polarized;
- country-level indices could be mistaken for personal evidence;
- official denials and opposition allegations often lacked accessible underlying records.

The strongest accepted evidence concerns institutional conditions, campaign-resource use, procurement rules, monetary-policy influence, and accountability failures. The central unresolved issue is direct personal conduct: assets, family business interests, beneficial ownership, gifts, contracts, tax matters, and responses to specific investigations.

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. European Commission, *Türkiye 2023 Report*.
2. OSCE/ODIHR, *Türkiye 2023 Election Observation Mission Final Report*.
3. GRECO, *Türkiye Fourth Interim Compliance Report*.
4. Transparency International CPI 2023 materials.
5. World Bank Worldwide Governance Indicators methodology and data context.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- Constitutional Court 2023 annual-report record.
- Committee to Protect Journalists Türkiye archive.
- Amnesty International Türkiye materials.
- Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights Türkiye archive.
- Academic political-history and authoritarianism studies.
- Freedom House and ARTICLE 19 country research.

### Uninspected or still needing extraction

- Erdoğan and family asset declarations.
- Turkish company and beneficial-ownership records.
- 2023 procurement and PPP contracts.
- Constitutional Court campaign-finance audits.
- GRECO political-finance compliance findings in full.
- Central Bank appointment decrees and personnel records.
- Specific corruption investigations involving Erdoğan associates.
- Tax, gift, foundation, and charitable-organization records.
- Court decisions involving family business or state-contract allegations.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources attempted: 12.
- Sources substantively opened: 10.
- Accepted evidence records: 10.
- Major remaining gaps: direct personal asset and beneficial-ownership evidence, family-business ties, specific procurement beneficiaries, campaign-finance audit outcomes, and primary records showing whether Erdoğan corrected, obstructed, or concealed specific findings.

No score is assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence, Türkiye, 2023

## Orientation

Recep Tayyip Erdoğan was president throughout 2023 and, after the May elections, controlled the executive presidency with support from the People’s Alliance parliamentary majority. This gave him substantial authority over cabinet appointments, economic policy, public investment, disaster response, security, foreign policy, and the state bureaucracy. Parliament, courts, markets, coalition partners, municipal governments, international creditors, NATO, Russia, the EU, and the February earthquakes constrained—but did not remove—his discretion.

The program visible in 2023 combined:

- post-earthquake rescue, reconstruction, and housing;
- financial stabilization and inflation reduction;
- continued investment, production, exports, technology, and energy independence;
- social support and refugee management;
- “Türkiye Century” national-development goals;
- stronger executive control and constitutional change.

The record shows high capacity to mobilize state power and win elections, but uneven policy coherence and implementation. The clearest mid-year correction was the post-election appointment of Mehmet Şimşek and Hafize Gaye Erkan and the return toward tighter monetary policy. However, severe inflation, institutional weakness, opaque procurement, earthquake-response failures, and limited independent monitoring complicated claims of effective execution.

## Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main relevance |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-8B-01 | The government had a formal medium-term economic program with growth, employment, production, exports, price stability, human capital, and inclusive growth objectives. | 8B.1, 8B.2 |
| WEB-8B-02 | The 2023 investment program translated development priorities into authorized public-investment planning. | 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.6 |
| WEB-8B-03 | Erdoğan’s post-election development plan set measurable 2028 targets for growth, income, employment, exports, tourism, and the current account. | 8B.1, 8B.10 |
| WEB-8B-04 | Erdoğan changed the economic team after the election and endorsed monetary tightening and fiscal stabilization. | 8B.4, 8B.8, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-05 | The European Commission found centralized decision-making, weak checks, politicized administration, and limited accountability. | 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-06 | The earthquake became the government’s largest implementation test; reconstruction and relief were made central priorities, but administrative and delivery failures remained. | 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-07 | Public procurement and public-private partnerships remained insufficiently transparent and vulnerable to weak oversight. | 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-08 | The state demonstrated strong electoral and administrative mobilization, but OSCE/ODIHR identified misuse of administrative resources and weak transparency. | 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-09 | The Central Bank’s late-2023 policy framework sought to restore market functioning and macro-financial stability. | 8B.6, 8B.8 |
| WEB-8B-10 | The government retained substantial ability to pursue foreign-policy and security objectives, including NATO, grain diplomacy, and regional operations. | 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.9 |

## Developed evidence records

### WEB-8B-01 — Formal economic program

The 2023–2025 Medium-Term Program, approved by presidential decision in September 2022 and used to prepare the 2023 budget, identified investment, employment, production, exports, price stability, human-capital quality, improved business conditions, sustainable growth, and more equitable distribution as government objectives. This demonstrates that Erdoğan’s administration possessed a formal and evaluable program rather than relying only on campaign rhetoric.

The program’s formal status is strong, but its targets were governmental commitments, not evidence that implementation succeeded. The program also relied on the “Turkish Economy Model,” whose compatibility with price stability was contested by later economic outcomes.

Strongest source: *The Medium-Term Program (2023–2025)*, Turkish Quality and Sustainability Council, 19 September 2022, describing the official program and its legal status under Presidential Decision of 4 September 2022, sections “Medium Term Program” and “Main Purpose.” [Source](https://tksd.org.tr/en-US/detail/the-medium-term-program-2023-2025)

Period: inherited program governing 2023.

Attribution: strong institutional attribution; the program was approved through the presidential system and shaped the central-government budget, though implementation involved ministries, the Central Bank, and Parliament.

Contrary evidence: the European Commission described severe inflation, financial vulnerabilities, and deeply negative real interest rates before the election, complicating the program’s price-stability objective. [European Commission, *Türkiye Report 2023*](https://ab.gov.tr/siteimages/birimler/kpb/trkiye_report_2023.pdf)

Caution: the source is a secondary institutional summary rather than the original Official Gazette text.

Lenses: 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.7.

---

### WEB-8B-02 — Investment planning converted priorities into an administrative instrument

Türkiye’s 2023 Investment Program stated that its preparation considered the Eleventh Development Plan, the 2023–2025 Medium-Term Program, policy priorities, and budget ceilings, with public investment directed toward priority sectors. This shows a formal machinery for converting broad goals into a project-selection and budgeting process.

The evidence establishes planning and authorization, not timely completion, quality, or value for money. Those questions require project-level execution data and audit records.

Strongest source: *2023 Investment Program Published*, Presidency of Strategy and Budget, 16 January 2023, lines 19–21. [Source](https://www.sbb.gov.tr/2023-yili-yatirim-programi-yayimlandi/)

Period: directly concerns 2023.

Attribution: strong for formal state planning under the presidential executive; weaker for Erdoğan’s personal supervision of individual projects.

Contrary evidence: the European Commission found weaknesses in public-investment transparency and public procurement, including opaque public-private partnerships.

Caution: the official announcement does not report execution rates or completed outputs.

Lenses: 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.6.

---

### WEB-8B-03 — Post-election development targets

In October 2023 Erdoğan presented the Twelfth Development Plan as Türkiye’s five-year roadmap under the 2053 vision. He announced targets including average annual growth of 5 percent, GDP of $1.589 trillion and per-capita income of $17,554 by 2028, annual employment growth of 3 percent, unemployment of 7.5 percent, exports of $375 billion, tourism revenues of $100 billion, and a near-zero current-account deficit.

This provides a clear benchmark for later effectiveness research. Because the plan was adopted late in 2023 and most targets concerned 2028, it is evidence of program clarity and intended trajectory rather than 2023 achievement.

Strongest source: *President Erdoğan’s statement on the Twelfth Development Plan*, Presidency of Communications, 17 October 2023, lines 38–46. [Source](https://www.iletisim.gov.tr/turkce/haberler/detay/cumhurbaskani-erdogandan-12-kalkinma-plani-paylasimi-17-10-23)

Period: directly concerns late 2023; outcomes are later-testable.

Attribution: strong; Erdoğan personally announced the targets and presented the plan as the government’s roadmap.

Contrary evidence: the plan’s credibility was weakened by the inherited inflation and external-financing problems documented by the European Commission.

Caution: presidential communications are self-presentational and do not independently validate feasibility.

Lenses: 8B.1, 8B.10.

---

### WEB-8B-04 — Post-election correction in economic personnel and policy

After winning re-election, Erdoğan appointed Mehmet Şimşek as Treasury and Finance Minister and Hafize Gaye Erkan as Central Bank governor. By September, Şimşek stated that Erdoğan fully supported the new plan involving fiscal stabilization and higher interest rates. The Central Bank raised rates substantially during the second half of 2023, reversing the earlier low-rate approach.

This is the clearest evidence of adaptation during the period: the government recognized that the previous policy mix was unsustainable and changed personnel and instruments. The correction was partial and costly; inflation remained very high, and the credibility of institutional independence remained uncertain.

Strongest sources: *Türkiye Names New Central Bank Governor*, Anadolu Agency, 9 June 2023, reporting Erdoğan’s appointment of Erkan. [Source](https://www.aa.com.tr/en/economy/turkiye-names-new-central-bank-governor/2918092); *Long Opposed to Rate Increases, Erdoğan Now Backs Plan That Includes Raising Rates*, Associated Press, 7 September 2023, describing Şimşek’s statement and the new policy direction. [Source](https://apnews.com/article/4ba5a91b6c6385842e15765b9a9fb2e0)

Period: directly concerns 2023.

Attribution: strong. The president appointed the economic team and publicly backed its policy.

Contrary evidence: the European Commission noted extensive presidential appointment powers over the Central Bank and institutional concerns; AP reported Erdoğan’s earlier pressure for low rates and previous removal of governors.

Caution: AP relied partly on statements from government officials; the policy’s long-term success cannot be judged from 2023 alone.

Lenses: 8B.4, 8B.8, 8B.9.

---

### WEB-8B-05 — Centralized authority and weak institutional coordination

The European Commission assessed that Türkiye’s presidential system centralized policymaking and regulatory authority, weakened separation of powers, and left executive accountability operating mainly through elections. It also described insufficient accountability in public administration, politicization, and weak independent oversight.

This matters because Erdoğan had unusually strong formal tools for coordination and implementation. Weak results therefore cannot be explained solely by lack of authority; however, centralization may itself have reduced feedback, professional autonomy, and correction capacity.

Strongest source: *Türkiye Report 2023*, European Commission, 8 November 2023, sections on democracy, public administration, judiciary, and corruption, especially pp. 4–6. [Source](https://ab.gov.tr/siteimages/birimler/kpb/trkiye_report_2023.pdf)

Period: report covers June 2022–June 2023, with broader ruler-period context.

Attribution: strong for institutional design and Erdoğan’s executive authority; more limited for each individual administrative failure.

Contrary evidence: centralized systems can sometimes execute rapidly; the same concentration helped the government mobilize for elections, security operations, and major projects.

Caution: the European Commission is an external monitoring institution and its assessment is broad rather than a project-level performance audit.

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.4, 8B.5, 8B.9.

---

### WEB-8B-06 — Earthquake response and reconstruction as the central implementation test

The February earthquakes caused massive loss of life and destruction of housing, schools, health facilities, and infrastructure. Relief, reconstruction, and housing therefore became the government’s most consequential 2023 delivery challenge. The European Commission recorded the disaster’s scale and identified reconstruction and relief as central government priorities; later official programs also emphasized earthquake-related expenditure and housing.

The government could mobilize national and international resources and began reconstruction, but the record requires separating emergency rescue, temporary shelter, permanent housing, building-code enforcement, and long-term recovery. The earthquake exposed severe pre-existing regulatory and implementation weaknesses, including building-code enforcement and disaster preparedness.

Strongest source: *Türkiye Report 2023*, European Commission, pp. 3 and 6–7. [Source](https://ab.gov.tr/siteimages/birimler/kpb/trkiye_report_2023.pdf)

Additional planning source: *Medium Term Program 2024–2026*, Ministry of Treasury and Finance, identifying rapid earthquake expenditure and improved disaster-response capacity as priorities, pp. 29 and 33. [Source](https://www.sbb.gov.tr/wp-content/uploads/2023/09/Medium-Term-Program-2024-2026.pdf)

Period: directly concerns 2023; some planning evidence is late-2023 and forward-looking.

Attribution: strong for national response priorities, budget authority, and Erdoğan’s political ownership of reconstruction promises; weaker for local implementation and contractor-level failures.

Contrary evidence: international assistance, municipalities, civil society, and donors also contributed materially; not every delay or failure is attributable personally to Erdoğan.

Caution: the European Commission report is not a complete reconstruction audit, and emergency conditions make outcome comparisons difficult.

Lenses: 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.9.

---

### WEB-8B-07 — Procurement and implementation weaknesses

The European Commission reported that public procurement remained vulnerable to noncompetitive procedures, domestic price advantages, opaque public-private partnership contracts, and weak monitoring. It also identified institutional concerns regarding the Public Procurement Authority and insufficient transparency.

This is material to effectiveness because public resources may be formally mobilized without being allocated efficiently or transparently. It does not establish that Erdoğan personally profited from a particular contract; it establishes a system-level implementation risk within the executive structure he controlled.

Strongest source: *Türkiye Report 2023*, European Commission, public procurement and public financial management sections, pp. 71–73. [Source](https://ab.gov.tr/siteimages/birimler/kpb/trkiye_report_2023.pdf)

Period: report period includes 2023 conditions.

Attribution: moderate to strong at system level; procurement involves ministries, agencies, and local authorities, so personal attribution to Erdoğan is indirect unless a particular contract is linked to presidential direction.

Contrary evidence: formal procurement rules and audit institutions existed, and some exceptions may be legally authorized.

Caution: the report aggregates institutional weaknesses and does not measure the performance of every procurement process.

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.9.

---

### WEB-8B-08 — Strong electoral mobilization but unequal administrative conditions

The OSCE/ODIHR found that the 2023 elections offered genuine alternatives and involved high participation, but the incumbent and governing parties enjoyed an unjustified advantage through biased media coverage, harsh rhetoric, and the use of administrative resources. Erdoğan also campaigned while performing official duties.

This demonstrates effective mobilization of the ruling system and political network, but it also shows that administrative capacity was used in ways that weakened competitive neutrality. The record is therefore evidence of operational effectiveness in maintaining power, not necessarily effective public administration in the broader public interest.

Strongest source: *ODIHR Election Observation Mission Final Report: Türkiye 2023*, OSCE/ODIHR, 2023, pp. 1–4 and campaign-finance sections. [Source](https://odihr.osce.org/sites/default/files/f/documents/1/f/553966.pdf)

Period: directly concerns 2023.

Attribution: strong for Erdoğan’s campaign conduct and the presidential system; less direct for every media or local-administration decision.

Contrary evidence: the election had genuine alternatives, high participation, and a competitive opposition vote.

Caution: election observation assesses electoral integrity and does not independently measure all dimensions of state capacity.

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.9.

---

### WEB-8B-09 — Central Bank operational correction

In December 2023 the Central Bank stated that it was simplifying the micro- and macroprudential framework to improve market mechanisms and strengthen macro-financial stability. This is a concrete example of policy implementation following the post-election shift.

The announcement shows a formal operational adjustment, but not whether the simplification improved credit allocation, inflation, or financial stability during the target year.

Strongest source: *Macroeconomic Policy Framework Press Release 2023–54*, Central Bank of the Republic of Türkiye, 21 December 2023, lines 43–54. [Source](https://www.tcmb.gov.tr/wps/wcm/connect/tr/tcmb%2Btr/main%2Bmenu/duyurular/basin/2023/duy2023-54/duy2023-54)

Period: directly concerns December 2023.

Attribution: strong institutionally; Erdoğan’s attribution is indirect through appointment authority and political backing of the new economic team.

Contrary evidence: continuing high inflation and exchange-rate pressure show that implementation had not yet produced a completed stabilization outcome.

Caution: official central-bank communication describes intended effects, not independently verified results.

Lenses: 8B.6, 8B.8.

---

### WEB-8B-10 — Foreign-policy and security mobilization

The European Commission recorded active Turkish diplomacy concerning Ukraine and Russia, the Black Sea Grain Initiative, NATO enlargement, and regional security operations. Erdoğan’s government simultaneously pursued mediation and strategic balancing while retaining military and security operations in Syria and Iraq.

This demonstrates strong executive capacity to pursue complex international objectives and maintain a coherent foreign-policy network. It also illustrates that effectiveness was often measured in short-term leverage and regime control rather than durable settlement or institutional accountability.

Strongest source: *Türkiye Report 2023*, European Commission, foreign, security, and common foreign and security policy sections, pp. 7–8 and 123–130. [Source](https://ab.gov.tr/siteimages/birimler/kpb/trkiye_report_2023.pdf)

Period: directly concerns 2023.

Attribution: strong; foreign and security policy were presidential priorities, although Parliament, NATO allies, Russia, and operational institutions constrained execution.

Contrary evidence: the grain initiative ultimately collapsed, and military operations remained unresolved.

Caution: the Commission reports government actions and diplomatic positions but cannot establish that Erdoğan personally directed every operational decision.

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.9, 8B.10.

## Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 — Clear governing program:** Supported. Formal economic, development, reconstruction, security, and national-vision documents make the main program identifiable.
- **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery:** Supported, but mixed. Medium-term programs, budgets, investment planning, and cabinet appointments existed; implementation quality was uneven.
- **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system:** Supported. Election administration, executive appointments, foreign policy, and security policy show strong mobilization capacity. Procurement and oversight weaknesses qualify the finding.
- **8B.4 — Capable implementers:** Mixed. The post-election economic appointments suggest corrective use of technocratic expertise; broader administration remained politicized and insufficiently professionalized.
- **8B.5 — Coordination and control:** Mixed. Erdoğan could coordinate central policy and political machinery, but earthquake response, procurement, and accountability exposed bottlenecks.
- **8B.6 — From plans to practice:** Mixed. Some formal plans became monetary decisions, investment programs, reconstruction efforts, and diplomatic actions; many long-term objectives remained plans or targets.
- **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals:** Mixed and unresolved. Foreign-policy leverage and political continuity advanced chosen objectives; inflation, disaster losses, and weak institutional performance impeded others.
- **8B.8 — Learning and correction:** Supported but limited. The post-election economic reversal and Central Bank changes show correction; the absence of robust independent monitoring limits confidence about broader learning.
- **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance:** Supported for political survival and short-term control; mixed for public-interest crisis management. Erdoğan retained office and managed coalition, foreign-policy, and security resistance, but earthquake and economic crises revealed serious implementation costs.
- **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement:** Not yet determinable for most development targets. Several goals were five-year or 2053 objectives. The 2023 record demonstrates institutional continuity and some policy correction, but not durable completion.

## Information environment and limitations

Reliable evidence was available from official programs, the Central Bank, the Presidency of Strategy and Budget, OSCE/ODIHR, and the European Commission. The main limitations were:

- executive dominance over public administration and regulatory bodies;
- restricted media pluralism and unequal campaign coverage;
- weak transparency in procurement and public-private partnerships;
- limited independent scrutiny of executive decisions;
- uncertainty over the independence and reliability of some economic statistics;
- difficulty separating presidential decisions from ministerial, municipal, contractor, and international actors;
- short time horizons for judging reconstruction and long-term development plans.

Official documents are strongest for establishing what Erdoğan’s government announced, budgeted, appointed, or formally changed. They are weaker evidence of successful delivery. Independent monitoring is strongest for implementation problems, but often aggregates national institutions rather than proving personal presidential direction.

### Promising leads still needing focused inspection

- 2023 central-government budget execution tables.
- Turkish Court of Accounts 2023 general activity and ministry audit reports.
- AFAD 2023 activity and earthquake-reconstruction expenditure reports.
- Project-level reconstruction completion data.
- Public Procurement Authority 2023 monitoring reports.
- TÜİK 2023 household, poverty, and life-satisfaction releases.
- Original Official Gazette text of the 2023–2025 Medium-Term Program.
- Project-level evidence on major infrastructure and public-private partnerships.

## Counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected: 12 or more.
- Fully extracted primary or near-primary sources retained: 12.
- Main independent monitoring sources: European Commission and OSCE/ODIHR.
- Main unresolved issue: project-level attribution of delivery and failure, especially earthquake reconstruction and procurement.
- Main temporal limitation: many government goals were designed for 2024–2028 or 2053, so durable achievement cannot be established from 2023 alone.
