--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

# Chapter 1B continuation — Nuclear and Existential Risk, Türkiye, 2023

The continuation materially improves the record on safeguards infrastructure, Akkuyu oversight, regulatory capacity, and cyber-response institutions. It does not establish a complete independent safety-performance record for 2023. No reliable evidence was found proving a major nuclear accident, safeguards violation, or catastrophic cyber incident; that absence is not evidence of safety because reporting and inspection systems are only partly transparent.

## New evidence records

### WEB-1B-08 — National safeguards and Akkuyu verification infrastructure

Türkiye’s Nuclear Regulatory Authority (NDK) reported that it conducts national safeguards inspections, checks nuclear-material inventories, supports IAEA safeguards, and operates training and safeguards-implementation projects. For Akkuyu, Türkiye submitted design-information questionnaires to the IAEA, participated in bilateral and trilateral working groups, and planned containment-and-surveillance systems for IAEA safeguards.

This is stronger than a formal treaty-status claim: it documents operational safeguards procedures and timely information submissions. It still does not independently prove that safeguards worked perfectly in practice or that all inspections found no irregularities.

Strongest source: *Enhancing National Safeguards Infrastructure to Support the Introduction of Nuclear Power*, IAEA Nuclear Energy Series, 2023, pp. 61–63, especially the sections on NDK inspections, Akkuyu design-information submissions, and safeguards working groups. [IAEA source](https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/PUB2063-Web.pdf)

Period: broader ruler-period and directly relevant to the 2023 Akkuyu construction phase.

Attribution: moderate to strong. NDK and the Turkish state controlled the safeguards system; Erdoğan’s personal responsibility is indirect through executive authority and appointments.

Contrary evidence: the same source records that Türkiye’s safeguards infrastructure was still being developed for a new nuclear program. Earlier IAEA review recommendations concerned physical protection, export controls, and illicit-trafficking procedures.

Caution: the source describes Turkish implementation and is not an independent 2023 inspection finding.

Lenses: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.6, 1B.7, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-09 — Nuclear regulatory capacity and incomplete rule development

A November 2023 presentation by Türkiye’s nuclear regulator stated that NDK regulated safety, security, and safeguards; that existing regulations were being revised using IAEA requirements and findings from a 2022 IAEA IRRS mission; and that 35 regulations were planned or under revision, with 14 updated and 21 still outstanding. It also described project-management plans and a multistep licensing process revised in light of Akkuyu experience.

This provides direct evidence of institutional construction and regulatory learning. It also shows that the regulatory framework was incomplete during the target period, which limits any conclusion that Türkiye had a fully mature nuclear-safety regime.

Strongest source: *Türkiye Nuclear Infrastructure, Safety Regime and Licensing Process*, NDK presentation to an IAEA regional workshop, November 2023, pp. 13–18. [IAEA-hosted presentation](https://gnssn.iaea.org/main/ansn/Activity%20Documents%20%20Public/Regional%20Workshop%20on%20Review%20and%20Assessment%20of%20Nuclear%20Power%20Plany%20License%20Applications%20by%20the%20Regulatory%20Bodies/%28D1-2%29%20TR%20Regulatory%20Framework%20and%20Licensing.pdf)

Period: directly concerns November 2023.

Attribution: strong for the national regulatory structure; personal attribution to Erdoğan is institutional rather than evidence of his direct technical decisions.

Contrary evidence: Türkiye had a legally defined regulator, multiple licensing stages, and IAEA-informed revisions.

Caution: the presentation is an official self-description and does not independently evaluate whether the regulations were enforced effectively.

Lenses: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-10 — Independent technical inspection of Akkuyu construction

The Turkish Standards Institution stated that NDK had authorized it as a technical-support organization for Akkuyu and that it conducted graded inspections of construction, site work, assembly, manufacturing, supplier competence, materials, and complex or first-of-a-kind components.

This is concrete evidence that Akkuyu was subject to a technical inspection mechanism beyond the project company itself. It does not establish that inspections were independent from political influence, that all deficiencies were corrected, or that the plant was safe to operate.

Strongest source: *Nuclear Structure Inspections*, Turkish Standards Institution, published 29 March 2023 and updated 24 August 2023, sections describing TSE’s NDK authorization and Akkuyu inspections. [TSE source](https://www.tse.org.tr/nukleer-yapi-denetimleri/)

Period: directly concerns 2023.

Attribution: moderate. Erdoğan’s government created and empowered the regulatory framework, but the source does not show presidential involvement in individual inspection decisions.

Contrary evidence: construction was still ongoing, and the project’s Russian ownership/operator structure complicates national control and accountability.

Caution: TSE is a state institution and its page reports its own role; no independent inspection results or deficiency register is published.

Lenses: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-11 — Cybersecurity strategy implementation and reported defensive activity

Türkiye’s 2020–2023 National Cybersecurity Strategy and Action Plan contained 40 actions and 75 sub-actions across eight strategic areas, including critical infrastructure protection, risk analysis, national incident response, and expansion of USOM/TR-CERT coordination. The BTK’s 2023 activity reporting was later summarized by Anadolu Agency as recording more than 104,000 malicious links detected and blocked through USOM and sectoral or institutional response teams.

This supports the existence of a national cyber-response architecture and measurable defensive activity. It does not establish that critical infrastructure was secure, that blocked links corresponded to prevented successful attacks, or that incident reporting was complete and independently audited.

Strongest sources: *National Cyber Security Strategy and Action Plan 2020–2023*, Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, sections describing 40 actions and 75 sub-actions. [Strategy PDF](https://hgm.uab.gov.tr/uploads/pages/strateji-eylem-planlari/national-cyber-security-strategy-2020-2023.pdf); *Türkiye’s Cyber Army Blocked More Than 104,000 Malicious Links*, Anadolu Agency, 2024 summary of the BTK 2023 activity report. [Anadolu Agency](https://www.aa.com.tr/tr/bilim-teknoloji/turkiyenin-siber-ordusu-gecen-yil-104-binden-fazla-zararli-baglantiya-gecit-vermedi/3327195)

Period: strategy inherited into and completed during 2023; numerical activity claim concerns 2023.

Attribution: moderate to strong at state-policy level. Erdoğan’s government established the executive cybersecurity architecture, but the evidence does not show his personal direction of incident response.

Contrary evidence: independent digital-news reporting documented internet restrictions after the February earthquake, which reduced access to information and complicated public scrutiny. [Reuters Institute, *Digital News Report 2023: Turkey*](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/digital-news-report/2023/turkey)

Caution: the 104,000 figure is government-derived and reported by a state-aligned news agency; it is not an independently audited outcome measure.

Lenses: 1B.1, 1B.9, 1B.10.

## Updated lens assessment

- **1B.1 — Reducing existential risk:** Improved but incomplete. Türkiye built nuclear safeguards and cybersecurity institutions and participated in IAEA processes. The evidence does not establish the overall effect on catastrophic risk.
- **1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** No material new evidence. The existing record remains primarily diplomatic and formal; no verified reckless nuclear threat was located in this continuation.
- **1B.3 — Safe nuclear control:** Improved. NDK inspections, TSE technical support, licensing procedures, and IAEA safeguards coordination are documented. Independent performance outcomes remain unavailable.
- **1B.4 — Arms control and inspections:** Improved. IAEA design-information submissions, safeguards working groups, and national inspections are documented.
- **1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression:** No new evidence linking Türkiye’s nuclear program to conventional aggression or domestic repression.
- **1B.6 — Preventing proliferation:** Partially improved. National safeguards and IAEA reporting systems are documented; independent evidence on export-control enforcement and illicit-trafficking investigations remains limited.
- **1B.7 — Independent risk expertise:** Partially improved. NDK, TSE, IAEA cooperation, and technical working groups show expert capacity. Their practical independence from executive and project pressures remains unresolved.
- **1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation:** No material new nuclear-crisis evidence located.
- **1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks:** Improved for cyber institutions and defensive activity, but no independent incident dataset or critical-infrastructure audit was found.
- **1B.10 — Lasting risk posture:** Unresolved. Türkiye left 2023 with more formal safeguards and regulatory machinery, but the framework was still developing and independent outcome evidence was insufficient.

## Source disposition

### Accepted and extracted

- IAEA, *Enhancing National Safeguards Infrastructure to Support the Introduction of Nuclear Power*.
- NDK, *Türkiye Nuclear Infrastructure, Safety Regime and Licensing Process*.
- Turkish Standards Institution, *Nuclear Structure Inspections*.
- Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, *National Cyber Security Strategy and Action Plan 2020–2023*.
- Anadolu Agency, *Türkiye’s Cyber Army Blocked More Than 104,000 Malicious Links*.

### Corroboration

- IAEA reports index and safeguards-report archive.
- International Energy Agency Türkiye energy-security profile.
- Council of Europe cybercrime country profile.
- Reuters Institute *Digital News Report 2023*.

### Remaining gaps

No accessible independent 2023 NDK inspection-result register, Akkuyu nonconformity log, parliamentary nuclear-safety inquiry, operator incident database, or independent critical-infrastructure cyber audit was located. The evidence therefore supports institutional capacity and formal implementation, but not a definitive conclusion about operational safety or cyber resilience.

The continuation was materially useful but not fully saturated because these primary records remain either unpublished, inaccessible, or not available in a searchable public archive.

--- SUPERVISOR RESEARCH TAKEOVER ROUND 2 ---

# Research continuation handoff — Chapter 1B

Completed a bounded continuation for the selected 1B chapter. I searched and opened IAEA, EU, Turkish regulator, Turkish Standards Institution, IEA, BTK, academic, and contrary-source materials. No score assigned.

## New accepted evidence units

### WEB-1B-12 — IAEA review identified unresolved national safety-policy gaps

- Claim: The IAEA’s IRRS review recognized Türkiye’s committed regulator but recommended that the government document a national nuclear/radiation-safety policy and strategy, clarify ministry roles, improve public participation, and continue updating emergency-preparedness rules.
- Source: [IAEA IRRS Final Report — Türkiye](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/documents/review-missions/final_irrs_report_turkiye.pdf)
- Locator: pp. 9, 15–16, and Appendix V, especially lines 259–261, 411–446, 448–462, and 3951–3959.
- Period fit: 2022 review; directly relevant to the regulatory baseline operating in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong institutional attribution to the government and NDK; Erdoğan’s responsibility is authority-based through the presidential executive system, not personal technical direction.
- Contrary evidence: The review also recognized NDK’s commitment and Türkiye’s nuclear-training programme.
- Lens links: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-13 — Türkiye’s Convention on Nuclear Safety report described Akkuyu safety systems and monitoring

- Claim: Türkiye’s national report described emergency-planning requirements, radiological-impact assessments, severe-accident analysis, public-exposure estimates, and an ongoing environmental monitoring programme for Akkuyu.
- Source: [Türkiye Convention on Nuclear Safety National Report](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/turkiye_cns_national_report_joint_8th_and_9th_rm.pdf)
- Locator: pp. 94–95, lines 6506–6572.
- Period fit: National report prepared for the Convention on Nuclear Safety review cycle; relevant to the 2023 construction and pre-operation phase.
- Attribution: State-level and regulatory attribution; Erdoğan’s role is indirect through national nuclear policy and executive institutions.
- Contrary evidence: The report is self-reported and states that monitoring was still ongoing; it does not independently verify the claimed performance.
- Lens links: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-14 — EU technical assistance documented capability-building and observed NDK inspections

- Claim: An EU nuclear-safety cooperation project supported NDK’s safety-analysis, construction-inspection, regulatory-management, and integrated-management capabilities. Its experts observed ten NDK inspections, including inspections at Akkuyu, and identified gaps or weaknesses against European and international standards.
- Source: [European Commission — Support to the Regulatory Authority of Türkiye](https://nuclear-safety-cooperation.ec.europa.eu/contracts/support-regulatory-authority-turkey-tr30116_en)
- Locator: lines 85–90, 105–135, 137–180.
- Period fit: Project evidence spanning the pre-2023 capacity-building period and directly relevant to NDK capability during 2023.
- Attribution: Strong institutional attribution to the Turkish regulatory system; no evidence of Erdoğan directing inspections.
- Contrary evidence: The project’s stated purpose was capacity enhancement because gaps and weaknesses remained; this is not evidence of mature independent performance.
- Lens links: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-15 — Akkuyu’s Russian ownership and transparency concerns

- Claim: Scholarly analysis identified legal criticism and public backlash concerning the Akkuyu international-agreement model, including the undisclosed project contract with Rosatom and limited transparency around the project’s governance.
- Source: [Türkiye’s Nuclear Energy Aspirations: Policy Challenges and Legal Trajectory](https://academic.oup.com/jwelb/article/17/3/201/7644944)
- Locator: discussion of the 2010 agreement, undisclosed project contract, and transparency criticism.
- Period fit: Inherited project structure with continuing relevance during 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for Erdoğan’s government’s strategic nuclear-policy direction; individual contracting decisions require primary records.
- Contrary evidence: The same analysis describes the 2022 Nuclear Regulatory Act as expanding NDK’s formal safety, security, safeguards, and inspection powers.
- Lens links: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-16 — 2023 earthquake revived Akkuyu seismic-safety controversy

- Claim: Contemporary legal-environmental reporting recorded that the 2023 earthquakes revived controversy over Akkuyu’s seismic setting. Environmental groups in Türkiye and Cyprus called for construction to stop, while the Ministry of Energy rejected reevaluation and Rosatom stated that the design could withstand a magnitude-9 earthquake.
- Source: [Yearbook of International Environmental Law — Turkey](https://academic.oup.com/yielaw/article/34/1/yvae044/7917567)
- Locator: 2023 Turkey entry, discussion of Akkuyu after the February earthquakes.
- Period fit: Direct 2023 contrary and risk-exposure evidence.
- Attribution: Strong for the government’s decision to continue the project; technical safety responsibility remains shared with NDK, Rosatom, contractors, and expert bodies.
- Contrary evidence: Government and Rosatom safety assurances are claims, not independent validation; the project’s seismic safety was not resolved by the inspected material.
- Lens links: 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-17 — 2023 cyber-response activity was measurable but government-derived

- Claim: BTK/USOM reporting indicated that in 2023 Türkiye detected and blocked more than 104,000 malicious links, issued more than 16,000 cybersecurity notifications, coordinated 14 sectoral and 2,205 institutional response teams, identified 205 vulnerabilities, assigned 204 CVE identifiers, and trained more than 498,000 users.
- Source: [Anadolu Agency summary of BTK’s 2023 activity report](https://www.aa.com.tr/tr/bilim-teknoloji/turkiyenin-siber-ordusu-gecen-yil-104-binden-fazla-zararli-baglantiya-gecit-vermedi/3327195)
- Locator: lines 46–77, especially 48, 56–64, and 67–77.
- Period fit: Direct 2023 activity evidence, published in 2024.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong at state-policy level; no evidence of Erdoğan’s personal direction of incident response.
- Contrary evidence: The figures are reported through a state-aligned news agency and do not establish prevented attacks, critical-infrastructure resilience, or independent audit quality.
- Lens links: 1B.1, 1B.9, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-18 — Independent academic account confirmed USOM’s institutional role

- Claim: A September 2023 Turkish academic article analyzed USOM as the central national cyber-incident-response institution and described it as part of Türkiye’s national cybersecurity-policy implementation.
- Source: [Gündoğdu, “Türkiye’nin Siber Güvenlik Politikası Uygulaması: USOM”](https://doi.org/10.18069/firatsbed.1261707)
- Locator: bibliographic record and article identification, volume 33(3), pp. 1325–1337.
- Period fit: Directly published in September 2023.
- Attribution: State-level institutional evidence; it does not establish operational success or presidential direction.
- Contrary evidence: The article is analytical rather than an independent incident-outcome dataset.
- Lens links: 1B.9, 1B.10.

## Updated chapter assessment

- 1B.1: Improved but mixed. Türkiye had formal safeguards, cyber-response institutions, EU-supported regulator capability-building, and documented risk-management procedures. The IAEA review and seismic controversy show unresolved safety-policy and transparency weaknesses.
- 1B.2: No material new evidence. No verified 2023 nuclear-use threat or reckless nuclear rhetoric was located.
- 1B.3: Improved but not closed. Regulatory responsibilities, inspections, safeguards procedures, emergency planning, and technical support are documented; inspection outcomes and deficiency correction remain unavailable.
- 1B.4: Stronger. The IAEA review and Convention on Nuclear Safety report provide more than treaty-status evidence, but implementation remains partly self-reported.
- 1B.5: No new evidence of nuclear cover for conventional aggression.
- 1B.6: Partially improved. Safeguards and inspection infrastructure are documented; export-control enforcement and illicit-trafficking outcomes remain unverified.
- 1B.7: Mixed. NDK had access to international expertise and EU technical assistance, but the IAEA identified incomplete policy development and unresolved public-participation and institutional-independence issues.
- 1B.8: No material new nuclear-crisis evidence.
- 1B.9: Improved for cyber governance and activity metrics, but the evidence does not establish critical-infrastructure resilience or incident outcomes.
- 1B.10: Unresolved. Türkiye ended 2023 with more formal safeguards, inspection, and cyber-response machinery, but also incomplete nuclear-policy development, Russian project dependence, transparency concerns, and weak independent outcome evidence.

## Searches attempted without recoverable additional evidence

I also searched for:

- independent 2023 NDK inspection-result registers;
- Akkuyu nonconformity and deficiency logs;
- Turkish parliamentary nuclear-safety inquiries;
- operator incident databases;
- independent critical-infrastructure cyber audits;
- verified 2023 cyber-incident outcome datasets;
- export-control and illicit-trafficking enforcement records.

No additional independently auditable record was recovered. The chapter remains continuation-worthy only for access-limited primary records, not because the existing evidence is absent.
